\begin{proposition}[An exact certificate with exempted positions]\label{prop:toprowcertS}
Let $r>1$, $L\ge2$, and let $S\subseteq Z\subset\N_{>0}$ with $|Z|=L-1$ and
$M=\max Z$.  Suppose that some real $p$ with $p(0)=1$ and
$\deg p\le M+1-L$ has the following property: with
$N(y)=p(y)(1-ry)^L=\sum_jN_jy^j$ and $g_z=\sum_{j\le z}N_j$, we have
$|g_z|\le1/\Phi_r(Z)$ for every $z\in[1,M+1]\setminus S$.  Then
$\mu_r(S,L)=\Phi_r(Z)$ and $V_r(L,|S|+1)\le1/\Phi_r(Z)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The construction in the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:toprowgen} uses only
$N(0)=1$ and the divisibility of $N$ by $(1-ry)^L$.  For each $K\ge M+1$ it
gives a monic multiple of $(x-r)^L$ whose nonleading coefficient at backward
distance $s$ is $g_s$ for $s\le K$ (with $g_s=g_{M+1}$ for $s>M+1$), and
at most $\beta+C/K$ in absolute value beyond, where
$\beta=\max_{z\notin S}|g_z|\le1/\Phi_r(Z)$.  The $|S|$ positions at the
distances in $S$ can carry at most the $|S|$ largest magnitudes, so
$b_{|S|+1}\le\beta+C/K$ for this multiple, and $V_r(L,|S|+1)\le1/\Phi_r(Z)$.
For any real $q$ with $\deg q\le L-1$, $q(0)=1$ and $q|_S=0$, the identity
of Proposition~\ref{prop:rowvalue}(a) for this multiple gives
$1\le(\beta+C/K)\Phi_r(q)$, so $\mu_r(S,L)\ge1/\beta\ge\Phi_r(Z)$; and
$\mu_r(S,L)\le\Phi_r(r_Z)=\Phi_r(Z)$.
\end{proof}

As for Proposition~\ref{prop:toprowcert}, $p$ is found from the
complementary slackness equations $g_s=-\beta\sgn r_Z(s)$ for
$s\in[1,M+1]\setminus Z$, whose solution has $\beta=1/\Phi_r(Z)$; the
positions in $S$ are not constrained.  For $S=\varnothing$ this is
Proposition~\ref{prop:toprowcert}.

\begin{corollary}[Single rows, including $r\le2$]\label{cor:rowstreevals}
Write $n=L-k+1$.  The conjectured value
$V_r(L,k)=\min_{1\le i\le k}1/\nu_i(n)$ holds in each of the following
cases, and the minimum is attained at the index $i^*$ stated.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] $r=2$ with $k=2$, $n\le14$; $k=3$, $n\le12$; $k=4$, $n\le8$;
$k=5$, $n\le5$; $k=6$, $n\le4$.  Here $i^*=1$, so
$V_2(L,k)=\beta_2(L-k+1)$; for instance every monic multiple of $(x-2)^4$
has $b_2\ge\frac{40}{11}$, and every monic multiple of $(x-2)^5$ has
$b_2\ge\frac{672}{115}$ and $b_3\ge\frac{40}{11}$.
\item[(b)] $r\in\{\frac{41}{20},\frac{21}{10}\}$ with $k=2$, $n\le12$ and
$k=3$, $n\le9$; here $i^*=1$.
\item[(c)] $r\in\{\frac{19}{10},\frac74,\frac32\}$ with $k=2$, $n\le12$;
$k=3$, $n\le9$; $k=4$, $n\le6$.  Here $i^*=1$, except $i^*=k$ for $n=2$ at
$r=\frac74$, and at $r=\frac32$ for $n\le4$ ($k=2$) and $n\le5$ ($k=3,4$).
\item[(d)] $r=\frac54$ with $k=2$, $n\le12$ ($i^*=2$ for $n\le10$ and
$i^*=1$ for $n=11,12$); $k=3$, $n\le9$ ($i^*=3$); $k=4$, $n\le4$ ($i^*=4$).
\item[(e)] $r=\frac{11}{10}$ with $k=2$, $n\le12$ ($i^*=2$); $k=3$,
$n\le 7$ ($i^*=3$).
\end{enumerate}

\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For each case we exhibit the data of Theorem~\ref{thm:rowstree} with
$\varphi=\Phi_r(Z^*)$, where $Z^*\supseteq[1,i^*-1]$ has $|Z^*|=n+i^*-2$ and
satisfies the hypothesis of Proposition~\ref{prop:toprowcertS} with
$S=[1,i^*-1]$ and $L=n+i^*-1$ (for $i^*=1$ this is
Proposition~\ref{prop:toprowcert}).  So $\varphi=\nu_{i^*}(n)$, and
Theorem~\ref{thm:rowstree} gives $V_r(L,k)\ge1/\nu_{i^*}(n)$, while
Theorem~\ref{thm:prefixrows} gives $V_r(L,k)\le\min_{i\le k}1/\nu_i(n)$.
Hence $V_r(L,k)=1/\nu_{i^*}(n)=\min_i1/\nu_i(n)$.  The root witness is a
top-row optimum $Z_0$ with $\Phi_r(Z_0)\le\varphi$; the witnesses $W_T$ are
found by a local search and every inequality $\Phi_r(W_T)\le\varphi$ is
checked in exact arithmetic, as are the certificates and the thresholds
$\theta_T$ (condition~\eqref{eq:farappend} at $\tau=\theta_T$, for some
$D$).  The largest tree has about $5.4\cdot10^5$ nodes ($r=2$, $k=6$,
$n=4$).
\end{proof}

At $r=2$ these rows sharpen Theorem~\ref{thm:everyrow}, since
$\beta_2(n)>n$ for $n\ge3$.  At $r=\frac32$ and $L=5$ all five rows are now
exact: the approximate values $2.98,\,1.99,\,0.80,\,0.20,\,\frac1{32}$
quoted in the paragraph on roots between one and two are
\[
  \frac{215233605}{72256243},\quad \frac{172186884}{86336203},\quad
  \frac{2657205}{3318724},\quad \frac{2302911}{11419568},\quad \frac1{32},
\]
the first by Proposition~\ref{prop:toprowcert} with $Z_0=\{2,5,9,16\}$, the
last by Corollary~\ref{cor:lastrowr}, and the others by (c), with
$i^*=k$ and $Z^*=\{1,4,9,16\}$, $\{1,2,8,15\}$, $\{1,2,3,13\}$.  In the cases computed at $r<2$ the minimizing
index is always $1$ or $k$: the worst exempted set is either the prefix
$[1,k-1]$ or a far block, as observed numerically after
Corollary~\ref{cor:belowtwo}, and the far block wins once $n$ is large
compared with $k$ (at $r=\frac{11}{10}$ this has not yet happened for
$n\le12$).  Theorem~\ref{thm:rowstree} treats one row at a time,
because Lemma~\ref{lem:farappend} needs a bound on the number of appended
zeros; for $r>2$, Theorems~\ref{thm:rowsfinite} and~\ref{thm:rowsrec} give
all $L$ at once.

\section{Complex roots}\label{sec:complexroots}

Everything so far prescribes real roots.  This section shows that complex
roots split into two regimes.  Roots that come in full orbits of
$x\mapsto\zeta x$, $\zeta^m=1$, over real values of $x^m$, in particular
purely imaginary conjugate pairs $\pm ic$, reduce \emph{exactly} to real
roots: the infimum of every order statistic and of $\Lambda$ over monic
multiples is the same as for the real roots $c^2$
(Theorem~\ref{thm:transfer}, Corollary~\ref{cor:imag}).  Without such
structure nothing beyond the first row survives: the first row holds at
every root set (Proposition~\ref{prop:rowone}), but the rows $k\ge2$ and every
mass bound of more than linear order fail for pairs lying within any fixed
angle of the imaginary axis (Proposition~\ref{prop:noangle}).  They hold
again, up to constant factors, once the moduli are separated by a factor $9$
(Corollary~\ref{cor:separated}).

\begin{lemma}[Multisection]\label{lem:multisection}
Let $m\ge1$, let $R\in\mathbb C[y]$ with $R(0)\ne0$, and put $P(x)=R(x^m)$.  Write
a polynomial $F\in\mathbb C[x]$ as
\[
  F(x)=\sum_{\varepsilon=0}^{m-1}x^\varepsilon E_\varepsilon(x^m),
  \qquad E_\varepsilon(y)=\sum_{i\ge0}f_{mi+\varepsilon}\,y^i .
\]
If $P$ divides $F$, then $R$ divides every $E_\varepsilon$.  If $F$ and $R$
are real, so are the $E_\varepsilon$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $\zeta=e^{2\pi i/m}$.  Since $P(\zeta^kx)=R(\zeta^{km}x^m)=P(x)$, the
polynomial $P$ divides $F(\zeta^kx)$ for every $k$, hence divides
\[
  \frac1m\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\zeta^{-k\varepsilon}F(\zeta^kx)
  =x^\varepsilon E_\varepsilon(x^m).
\]
As $R(0)\ne0$, $P(0)\ne0$, so $x^\varepsilon$ is coprime to $P$ and
$R(x^m)\mid E_\varepsilon(x^m)$.  Divide in $\mathbb C[y]$:
$E_\varepsilon=RQ+S$ with $\deg S<\deg R$.  Then $R(x^m)$ divides
$S(x^m)$, whose degree $m\deg S$ is smaller than $m\deg R$, so $S=0$.
Reality of $E_\varepsilon$ is clear from its coefficients.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Multisection transfer]\label{thm:transfer}
Let $m$, $R$ and $P=R(x^m)$ be as in Lemma~\ref{lem:multisection}, and let
$F$ be a nonzero multiple of $P$ of degree $D$.  For the residue
$\varepsilon\equiv D\pmod m$, the polynomial $E_\varepsilon$ is a multiple of
$R$ of degree $(D-\varepsilon)/m$ with leading coefficient $f_D$, and
\[
  b_k(F)\ge b_k(E_\varepsilon)\quad(k\ge1),\qquad
  \Lambda(F)\ge\Lambda(E_\varepsilon).
\]
Conversely, for every multiple $G$ of $R$ the polynomial $G(x^m)$ is a
multiple of $P$ with the same nonzero coefficients.  Consequently, for
$\Phi=b_k$ (any fixed $k$) and for $\Phi=\Lambda$,
\[
  \inf\{\Phi(F):F\text{ monic},\ P\mid F\}
  =\inf\{\Phi(G):G\text{ monic},\ R\mid G\},
\]
over complex multiples, and also over real multiples when $R$ is real.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By Lemma~\ref{lem:multisection}, $R\mid E_\varepsilon$.  The top coefficient
of $F$ sits at $D=m\cdot\frac{D-\varepsilon}m+\varepsilon$, so it is the
coefficient of $y^{(D-\varepsilon)/m}$ in $E_\varepsilon$, and no higher
coefficient of $E_\varepsilon$ is nonzero.  The nonleading coefficients of
$E_\varepsilon$ are the $f_j$ with $j\equiv\varepsilon$, $j<D$: a
sub-multiset of the nonleading coefficients of $F$.  The $k$-th largest
element of a sub-multiset is at most the $k$-th largest of the whole, and
$\Lambda$ is a sum of nonnegative terms over coefficients; this gives the
two inequalities.  Monicity passes from $F$ to $E_\varepsilon$, which gives
$\ge$ between the infima.  For the converse, $R\mid G$ gives
$R(x^m)\mid G(x^m)$, and $G(x^m)$ has the coefficients of $G$ spread out
with zeros between them, so $b_k$ and $\Lambda$ are unchanged, and
$G(x^m)$ is monic when $G$ is.  This gives $\le$.
\end{proof}

The case $m=2$, $R(y)=\prod_j(y+c_j^2)$ is the case of purely imaginary
conjugate pairs; the sign change $y\mapsto-y$ turns $R$ into a product of
linear factors at the positive roots $c_j^2$ without changing any
coefficient magnitude.

\begin{corollary}[Purely imaginary pairs]\label{cor:imag}
Let $c_1\le\cdots\le c_K$ be positive reals, repetitions allowed, put
$P_{\rm im}=\prod_{j=1}^K(x^2+c_j^2)$ and
$P_{\rm re}=\prod_{j=1}^K(y-c_j^2)$, and let $F\in\mathbb C[x]$ be a nonzero
multiple of $P_{\rm im}$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item If $c_1^2\ge2$, then for $1\le k\le K$,
\[
  b_k(F)\ \ge\ |f_D|\prod_{j=k}^K(c_j^2-1),
\]
and the $k$ magnitudes may be taken among the positions $j\equiv D\pmod2$.
The row $k=1$ holds for $c_1>1$.
\item If $c_1^2\ge2$ and $|f_D|\ge1$, then
\[
  \Lambda(F)\ \ge\ (K+1)\log|f_D|+\sum_{j=1}^Kj\log(c_j^2-1).
\]
\item For $\Phi=b_k$ and $\Phi=\Lambda$, the infimum of $\Phi$ over monic
(real or complex) multiples of $P_{\rm im}$ equals its infimum over monic
multiples of $P_{\rm re}$.  In particular, with $c_1^2\ge2$ and $K\ge2$,
\[
  \inf_{Q\text{ monic}}\Lambda(P_{\rm im}Q)\ \asymp\
  K^2+\sum_{j=1}^Kj\log(c_j^2-1)
\]
with the absolute constants of Corollary~\ref{cor:allroots};
$\Lambda(F)\ge K^2/80$ for monic $F$ when $K\ge8$; and the row $k=2$ is an
infimum at $(c_1^2,c_2^2)=(2,3)$, the rows $k=2,3$ at
$(c_1^2,c_2^2,c_3^2)=(2,3,5)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:transfer} with $m=2$ and $R(y)=\prod_j(y+c_j^2)$;
$R(0)\ne0$.  The multiple $E_\varepsilon$ of $R$ has leading coefficient
$f_D$, and $\tilde E(y)=E_\varepsilon(-y)$ is a multiple of $P_{\rm re}$
with leading coefficient $\pm f_D$ and the same coefficient magnitudes.  For
real $F$, Theorem~\ref{thm:order} (with Corollary~\ref{cor:complex} for complex $F$) applied to
$\tilde E$ at the roots $c_j^2\ge2$ gives item~1, since
$b_k(F)\ge b_k(\tilde E)$ and the positions of $\tilde E$ are those
$j\equiv D\pmod 2$ of $F$; the clause $c_1>1$ is the $u=0$ clause of
Theorem~\ref{thm:order}.  Item~2 is Corollary~\ref{cor:mass} applied to $\tilde E$, together with
$\Lambda(F)\ge\Lambda(\tilde E)$.  Item~3 is the equality of infima in
Theorem~\ref{thm:transfer}, composed with the magnitude-preserving
bijection $G(y)\mapsto\pm G(-y)$ between monic multiples of $R$ and of
$P_{\rm re}$; the three quoted statements are Corollary~\ref{cor:allroots},
Proposition~\ref{prop:quadratic} and Propositions~\ref{prop:sharp23} and~\ref{prop:sharp235} of the real-root theory at
the roots $c_j^2$.
\end{proof}

Thus a purely imaginary pair $\pm ic$ is worth exactly one real root $c^2$:
the pair has two roots of modulus $c$, and the mass charges $\log c^2$ once
at its rank.  This is the right count.  By item~3 the product
$P_{\rm im}$ itself is within $O(K^2)$ of the infimum
(Corollary~\ref{cor:near}), and $\Lambda(P_{\rm im})=\Lambda(P_{\rm re})$; so a
bound of the form $\sum_{i\le2K}i\log\rho_{(i)}$ over the $2K$ roots
counted separately, which would be about twice as large, is false already
for $P_{\rm im}$.

The same proof applies to every factor of the form $R(x^m)$ with $R$ real
and all roots of $R$ of one sign: the real roots $\pm r$ in pairs
($R=\prod(y-r_j^2)$), or the $m$-th roots of $\pm s_j$ for fixed $m$.  One
more reduction needs no multisection.

\begin{corollary}[Roots on a common ray]\label{cor:ray}
Let $\theta\in\R$ and $\rho_1\le\cdots\le\rho_K$ with $\rho_1\ge2$, and let
$F\in\mathbb C[x]$ be a nonzero multiple of $\prod_j(x-\rho_je^{i\theta})$.  Then
$b_k(F)\ge|f_D|\prod_{j\ge k}(\rho_j-1)$ for $1\le k\le K$.  In
particular this holds for a real $F$ divisible by
$\prod_j(x^2-2\rho_j\cos\theta\,x+\rho_j^2)$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
$G(x)=F(e^{i\theta}x)$ has coefficients $f_je^{ij\theta}$, of the same
magnitudes, and is divisible by $\prod_j(x-\rho_j)$; apply
Corollary~\ref{cor:complex}.
\end{proof}

A common ray charges each pair once, with $\log(\rho-1)$ in place of
$\log(\rho^2-1)$; the imaginary axis is special because it carries both
members of each pair.

\begin{proposition}[The first row at arbitrary roots]\label{prop:rowone}
Let $\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_N\in\mathbb C$, repetitions allowed, with
$\rho_j=|\alpha_j|$, and let $F\in\mathbb C[x]$ be a nonzero multiple of
$\prod_j(x-\alpha_j)$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item If every $\rho_j>1$, then $b_1(F)\ge|f_D|\prod_{j=1}^N(\rho_j-1)$.
\item For every $0<r<1$,
$b_1(F)\ge|f_D|\,\frac{1-r}{r}\bigl(r^N\mathcal M-1\bigr)$, where
$\mathcal M=\prod_j\max(1,\rho_j)$; with $r=N/(N+1)$,
$b_1(F)\ge|f_D|\,(\mathcal M/e-1)/N$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $F^*(t)=t^DF(1/t)=\sum_{d=0}^Df_{D-d}t^d$ and $\beta_j=1/\alpha_j$
where $\alpha_j\ne0$.

\emph{Item 1.}  Here every $\alpha_j\ne0$, and if $(x-\alpha)^\mu\mid F$
then $F^*(t)=(1-\alpha t)^\mu\,t^{D-\mu}H(1/t)$ with $F=(x-\alpha)^\mu H$, so
$\chi(t)=\prod_j(t-\beta_j)$ divides $F^*$.  Put
$B(s)=\prod_j(1-\beta_js)=s^N\chi(1/s)$, let $c=-[s^N]B$, and let
$v_d=[s^d]V$ for $V=(B+cs^N)/B=1+cs^N/B$.  The numerator $A=B+cs^N$ has
degree at most $N-1$, so $BV=A$ gives, for every $n\ge N$,
$\sum_{k=0}^N[s^k]B\;v_{n-k}=0$, that is,
$\sum_{i=0}^N\chi_iv_{m'+i}=0$ for every $m'\ge0$, where
$\chi=\sum_i\chi_it^i$.  Writing $F^*=\chi h$,
\[
  \sum_{d}v_df_{D-d}
  =\sum_{m'}h_{m'}\sum_i\chi_iv_{m'+i}=0 .
\]
Since $v_0=1$, $|f_D|\le b_1(F)\sum_{d\ge1}|v_d|$.  Now
$|c|=\prod_j|\beta_j|=\prod_j\rho_j^{-1}$, and the coefficients of $1/B$ are
dominated in modulus by those of $\prod_j(1-|\beta_j|s)^{-1}$, whose sum is
$\prod_j(1-\rho_j^{-1})^{-1}$.  Hence
$\sum_{d\ge1}|v_d|\le\prod_j\rho_j^{-1}(1-\rho_j^{-1})^{-1}
=\prod_j(\rho_j-1)^{-1}$.

\emph{Item 2.}  $F^*(0)=f_D\ne0$, and $F^*$ vanishes at every $\beta_j$
(counted with multiplicity, as in item 1).  Jensen's formula on $|t|\le r$
gives
\[
  \log\max_{|t|=r}|F^*|\ \ge\ \log|f_D|+\sum_{j:\rho_j>1/r}\log(r\rho_j)
  \ \ge\ \log|f_D|+\sum_{j}\log\bigl(r\max(1,\rho_j)\bigr),
\]
since each omitted or added term is $\le0$ on the right.  On the other side
$\max_{|t|=r}|F^*|\le|f_D|+b_1(F)\sum_{d\ge1}r^d
=|f_D|+b_1(F)\,r/(1-r)$.  Rearranging gives the first bound; for
$r=N/(N+1)$, $(1-r)/r=1/N$ and $r^N=(1+1/N)^{-N}\ge e^{-1}$.
\end{proof}

For real roots $r_j>1$ item 1 is the row $k=1$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:order}.  Both items
are of the size of the Mahler measure: they charge each root once, as
$\log\rho_j$, and do not give a second large coefficient.  At arbitrary
angles nothing more is true.

\begin{proposition}[No angle-uniform rows beyond the first]
\label{prop:noangle}
\leavevmode
\begin{enumerate}
\item Let $M\ge3$ be odd, $\rho>1$, and $0<\delta<\pi/2$.  The monic
polynomial $x^{2M}+\rho^{2M}$ is divisible by the product of the
$K=2\lfloor\delta M/\pi\rfloor+1$ conjugate pairs of modulus $\rho$ with
arguments $\pm(\frac\pi2+t\frac\pi M)$, $|t|\le\delta M/\pi$, all within
$\delta$ of the imaginary axis; yet
\[
  b_2(x^{2M}+\rho^{2M})=0,\qquad
  \Lambda(x^{2M}+\rho^{2M})=2M\log\rho\le\frac\pi\delta(K+1)\log\rho .
\]
\item For $m\ge2$ and $B\ge1$, the polynomial $x^{2m}+x+B$ has no real
root, so its roots form $m$ conjugate pairs, and $b_2=1$,
$\Lambda=\log B$.
\end{enumerate}
In particular, no bound $b_2(F)\ge\phi(\rho_1,\ldots,\rho_K)>0$ in terms of
the moduli of the prescribed pairs alone, not even for pairs within a fixed
angle $\delta$ of the imaginary axis, and no bound
$\Lambda(F)\ge c\,K^2\log\rho$ for $K$ pairs of modulus $\rho$ within a
fixed angle $\delta$, can hold for all monic multiples.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
1.  The roots of $x^{2M}+\rho^{2M}$ are $\rho e^{i\pi(2l+1)/(2M)}$; for
$M$ odd, $l=(M-1)/2+t$ gives the argument $\frac\pi2+t\frac\pi M$, and the
conjugate root has the negated argument.  The integers $t$ with
$|t|\le\delta M/\pi$ number $K$.  The only nonleading nonzero coefficient
is $\rho^{2M}$, so $b_2=0$ and $\Lambda=2M\log\rho$.  Finally
$K\ge2\delta M/\pi-1$ gives $2M\le\pi(K+1)/\delta$.

2.  For real $x$ with $|x|\ge1$, $x^{2m}+x\ge|x|^{2m}-|x|\ge0$, and for
$|x|<1$, $x^{2m}+x>-1$; so $x^{2m}+x+B>0$ when $B\ge1$.  The coefficients are
$1,1,B$.

For the final sentence, item 1 with $\delta M\ge\pi$, so $K\ge3$, has $b_2=0$ while every
$\rho_j=\rho$ is fixed, and letting $M\to\infty$ at fixed $\delta$ gives
$K\to\infty$ with $\Lambda=O_\delta(K\log\rho)$.
\end{proof}

Item 2 has distinct moduli: numerically $(2.1477,2.1545,2.1611)$ at
$(m,B)=(3,100)$.  So distinct moduli do not help either; what the
imaginary axis provides in Corollary~\ref{cor:imag} is not closeness of the
angles but exact membership, which makes $R(x^2)$ a polynomial in $x^2$.

Distinct moduli do not help, but separated moduli do.  The reason is the
Archimedean Newton polygon, the counterpart at the infinite place of the
$q$-adic polygons of Theorem~\ref{thm:padicmass} below.  For a nonzero
$F=\sum_if_ix^i\in\mathbb C[x]$ of degree $D$ and order $e$ at $0$, let
$\varphi$ be the least concave function on $[e,D]$ with
$\varphi(i)\ge\log|f_i|$ whenever $f_i\ne0$.  Its vertices
$D=v_0>v_1>\cdots>v_L=e$ are among these points, so $\varphi(v_t)=\log|f_{v_t}|$.
The edge joining $v_s$ to $v_{s-1}$ has length $\ell_s=v_{s-1}-v_s\ge1$ and
slope $-\sigma_s$; by concavity $\sigma_1>\sigma_2>\cdots>\sigma_L$, and
\[
  \log|f_{v_t}|=\log|f_D|+\sum_{s\le t}\ell_s\sigma_s\qquad(0\le t\le L).
\]
We call $\sigma_1,\ldots,\sigma_L$ the \emph{tropical roots} of $F$.  For
$u\in\R$ the vertex $v_s$ with $\sigma_{s+1}<u<\sigma_s$ (where
$\sigma_0=+\infty$ and $\sigma_{L+1}=-\infty$) is the one maximizing
$\log|f_i|+iu$; we call it \emph{active} at $u$.

\begin{theorem}[Archimedean Newton polygon]\label{thm:archnewton}
Let $F\in\mathbb C[x]$ be nonzero, with tropical roots $\sigma_1>\cdots>\sigma_L$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Let $u\in\R$ satisfy $|u-\sigma_s|>\log3$ for every $s$, let $v$ be the
vertex active at $u$, and $r=e^u$.  Then
$|f_v|r^v>\sum_{i\ne v}|f_i|r^i$.  Hence $F$ has no root on $|z|=r$ and
exactly $v$ roots, counted with multiplicity, in $|z|<r$.
\item Every nonzero root $\beta$ of $F$ satisfies
$\bigl|\log|\beta|-\sigma_s\bigr|\le\log3$ for some $s$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
1.  Let $v=v_s$, so $\sigma_{s+1}<u<\sigma_s$, and put $d_+=\sigma_s-u$ and
$d_-=u-\sigma_{s+1}$, both larger than $\log3$ (and infinite when $s=0$,
respectively $s=L$).  A concave function lies below the line through any of
its edges, so for $i>v$ the edge of slope $-\sigma_s$ gives
$\log|f_i|\le\varphi(i)\le\varphi(v)-\sigma_s(i-v)$, that is,
$|f_i|r^i\le|f_v|r^ve^{-(i-v)d_+}$; likewise the edge of slope $-\sigma_{s+1}$
gives $|f_i|r^i\le|f_v|r^ve^{-(v-i)d_-}$ for $i<v$.  Summing over the
distinct positive integers $|i-v|$,
\[
  \sum_{i\ne v}|f_i|r^i\ \le\ |f_v|r^v\Bigl(\frac1{e^{d_+}-1}+\frac1{e^{d_-}-1}\Bigr)
  \ <\ |f_v|r^v\Bigl(\frac12+\frac12\Bigr).
\]
So $|F(z)|\ge|f_v|r^v-\sum_{i\ne v}|f_i|r^i>0$ on $|z|=r$, and by Rouch\'e's
theorem $F$ and $f_vz^v$ have the same number of zeros in $|z|<r$, namely $v$.

2.  If $|\log|\beta|-\sigma_s|>\log3$ for every $s$, item~1 at
$u=\log|\beta|$ shows $F(\beta)\ne0$.
\end{proof}

Item~2 is the Archimedean form of the Newton polygon theorem: up to the
factor $3$, the moduli of the roots can only sit at the tropical roots.
Unlike the $q$-adic case, the roots of modulus near $e^{\sigma_s}$ need not
number $\ell_s$; $(x-1)^D$ has all its roots on $|z|=1$ but
tropical roots $\log\frac{i+1}{D-i}$ spread over $[-\log D,\log D]$.
Separated roots still force separated vertices, which is all the mass needs.

\begin{corollary}[Separated moduli]\label{cor:separated}
Let $F\in\mathbb C[x]$ be nonzero of degree $D$, and let
$\rho_1\le\cdots\le\rho_K$ be moduli of nonzero roots of $F$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item If $\rho_1\ge3$ and $\rho_{j+1}>9\rho_j$ for $1\le j<K$, there are
positions $y_1<\cdots<y_K<D$ with
\[
  |f_{y_j}|\ \ge\ |f_D|\prod_{i=j}^K\frac{\rho_i}3 .
\]
Hence $b_k(F)\ge|f_D|\prod_{i=k}^K(\rho_i/3)$ for $1\le k\le K$, and, if
$|f_D|\ge1$,
\[
  \Lambda(F)\ \ge\ (K+1)\log|f_D|+\sum_{j=1}^Kj\log\frac{\rho_j}3 .
\]
\item If moreover each $\rho_j$ is the modulus of two roots of $F$, counted
with multiplicity (for instance $\beta_j$ and $\bar\beta_j$ with $F$ real and
$\beta_j\notin\R$), and $\rho_1>27$ and $\rho_{j+1}>729\rho_j$, then
$b_k(F)\ge|f_D|\prod_{i=k}^K(\rho_i^2/729)$ for $1\le k\le K$, and, if
$|f_D|\ge1$,
\[
  \Lambda(F)\ \ge\ (K+1)\log|f_D|+\sum_{j=1}^Kj\log\frac{\rho_j^2}{729} .
\]
\item If $\rho_1\ge3$, $g\ge1$, and $\rho_{j+g}>9\rho_j$ for $1\le j\le K-g$,
then $b_k(F)\ge|f_D|\prod_{t=0}^{\lceil K/g\rceil-k}(\rho_{K-tg}/3)$ for
$1\le k\le\lceil K/g\rceil$, and, if $|f_D|\ge1$,
\[
  \Lambda(F)\ \ge\ \frac1g\sum_{j=1}^K\Bigl\lceil\frac jg\Bigr\rceil\log\frac{\rho_j}3
  \ \ge\ \frac1{g^2}\sum_{j=1}^Kj\log\frac{\rho_j}3 .
\]
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
1.  By Theorem~\ref{thm:archnewton}, item~2, choose for each $j$ an index
$s(j)$ with $|\sigma_{s(j)}-\log\rho_j|\le\log3$.  Since
$\log\rho_{j+1}-\log\rho_j>2\log3$,
\[
  \sigma_{s(j+1)}\ge\log\rho_{j+1}-\log3>\log\rho_j+\log3\ge\sigma_{s(j)},
\]
so $s(1)>s(2)>\cdots>s(K)\ge1$, and $y_j=v_{s(j)}$ are increasing positions
below $D$.  In
$\log|f_{y_j}|=\log|f_D|+\sum_{s\le s(j)}\ell_s\sigma_s$ every term has
$\ell_s\ge1$ and $\sigma_s\ge\sigma_{s(j)}\ge\log(\rho_j/3)\ge0$; keeping only
the terms $s=s(i)$, $i\ge j$, gives
$\log|f_{y_j}|\ge\log|f_D|+\sum_{i\ge j}\log(\rho_i/3)$.  Every factor
$\rho_i/3$ is at least $1$, so the $k$ positions $y_1,\ldots,y_k$ all carry
magnitudes at least $|f_D|\prod_{i\ge k}(\rho_i/3)$, which bounds $b_k(F)$.
For the mass, $\Lambda(F)\ge\log^+|f_D|+\sum_j\log^+|f_{y_j}|$, and
$\sum_j\sum_{i\ge j}\log(\rho_i/3)=\sum_ii\log(\rho_i/3)$.

2.  Fix $c$ with $3\log3<c<\log\rho_1$ and $2c<\log(\rho_{j+1}/\rho_j)$ for
every $j$, and let $W_j=[\log\rho_j-c,\log\rho_j+c]$.  These windows are
disjoint, lie in $(0,\infty)$, and $W_{j+1}$ lies above $W_j$.  We claim that
the tropical roots in $W_j$ have total multiplicity
$\sum_{\sigma_s\in W_j}\ell_s\ge2$.  By Theorem~\ref{thm:archnewton}, item~2,
$W_j$ contains a tropical root $\sigma_s$ with $|\sigma_s-\log\rho_j|\le\log3$.
If it contains another one, the claim holds.  Otherwise take
$0<\varepsilon<c-3\log3$ and $u_\pm=\sigma_s\pm(\log3+\varepsilon)$.  Both are
farther than $\log3$ from $\sigma_s$, and every other tropical root lies
outside $W_j$, at distance more than $c-(2\log3+\varepsilon)>\log3$ from
$u_\pm$, because $|u_\pm-\log\rho_j|\le2\log3+\varepsilon$.  No tropical root
other than $\sigma_s$ lies in $[u_-,u_+]\subset W_j$, so the vertices active
at $u_+$ and $u_-$ are $v_{s-1}$ and $v_s$, and by
Theorem~\ref{thm:archnewton}, item~1, $F$ has exactly
$v_{s-1}-v_s=\ell_s$ roots in $e^{u_-}<|z|<e^{u_+}$.  The two roots of modulus
$\rho_j$ are among them, as $|\log\rho_j-\sigma_s|\le\log3$; so $\ell_s\ge2$.

Now let $\sigma_{s(j)}$ be the smallest tropical root in $W_j$ and
$y_j=v_{s(j)}$.  In $\log|f_{y_j}|=\log|f_D|+\sum_{s\le s(j)}\ell_s\sigma_s$
every $\sigma_s$ is at least $\sigma_{s(j)}>0$, and the sum contains every
tropical root in $W_i$ for $i\ge j$; each of these is at least
$\log\rho_i-c$.  By the claim,
$\log|f_{y_j}|\ge\log|f_D|+2\sum_{i\ge j}(\log\rho_i-c)$.  As in item~1,
$b_k(F)\ge|f_D|\prod_{i\ge k}(\rho_i^2e^{-2c})$ and
$\Lambda(F)\ge(K+1)\log|f_D|+\sum_jj\log(\rho_j^2e^{-2c})$.  These hold for
every $c$ in a nonempty interval with infimum $3\log3$; letting
$c\downarrow3\log3$ gives the stated bounds, as $e^{6\log3}=729$.

3.  For $1\le r\le g$ the roots $\rho_j$ with $j\equiv r\pmod g$ satisfy the
hypotheses of item~1, and $\rho_j$ is the $\lceil j/g\rceil$-th of them.
Item~1 for the class of $K$ gives the rows.  For the mass, item~1 gives
$\Lambda(F)\ge\sum_{j\equiv r}\lceil j/g\rceil\log(\rho_j/3)$ for each $r$,
after dropping the terms $\log|f_D|\ge0$; average over $r$, and use
$\lceil j/g\rceil\ge j/g$ with $\log(\rho_j/3)\ge0$.
\end{proof}

So when the prescribed moduli grow by a factor $9$ at each step, every
complex multiple pays $\sum_jj\log(\rho_j/3)$, one real root $\rho_j/3$ per
root, at every angle; with a factor $729$ a conjugate pair pays
$\log(\rho_j^2/729)$, which is the charge $\log(c_j^2-1)$ of
Corollary~\ref{cor:imag} up to the constant.  Proposition~\ref{prop:noangle}
shows that some separation is necessary; the constants $3$, $9$ and $729$ are
not optimized.

\subsection{Real roots of both signs}\label{sec:bothsigns}

Theorem~\ref{thm:order} needs its roots on one side of $0$.  Applied to $F(x)$
and to $F(-x)$ it charges a multiple with $p$ positive and $n$ negative
prescribed roots only for the larger of the two classes.  The mixed problem is
genuinely weaker: by the multisection transfer (the remark after
Corollary~\ref{cor:imag}, with $R=\prod_j(y-s_j^2)$), the $L=2m$ roots $\pm s_j$
of $\prod_j(x^2-s_j^2)$ are worth only the $m$ positive roots $s_j^2$, so at a
common modulus $s$ they cost about $\frac14L^2\log s$ instead of the
$\frac12L^2\log s$ of $L$ roots of one sign.  Rolle's theorem, used on the two
half-lines separately as in Proposition~\ref{prop:quadratic}, gives the
matching lower bound: every excluded coefficient costs one root of each sign.

\begin{lemma}[Rolle on both half-lines]\label{lem:rolleside}
Let $h\in\R[t]$ be nonzero, let $z\ge1$ be an integer, and put
$T_zh=h-(t/z)h'$, so that $[t^d]T_zh=(1-d/z)[t^d]h$.  If $h$ has the zeros
$0<\xi_1\le\cdots\le\xi_m$, listed with multiplicity, $m\ge1$, then $T_zh$ has
zeros $\eta_1\le\cdots\le\eta_{m-1}$ in $(0,\infty)$, listed with multiplicity,
with $\xi_j\le\eta_j\le\xi_{j+1}$.  The same holds in $(-\infty,0)$ for the
absolute values: zeros $|\xi_1|\le\cdots\le|\xi_m|$ of $h$ there give zeros
$|\eta_1|\le\cdots\le|\eta_{m-1}|$ of $T_zh$ there with
$|\xi_j|\le|\eta_j|\le|\xi_{j+1}|$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For $t\ne0$, $T_zh=-(t^{z+1}/z)(t^{-z}h)'$.  On $I=(0,\infty)$ the function
$\varphi=t^{-z}h$ is real-analytic, not identically zero, and has the zeros of
$h$ with the same multiplicities.  Let $x_1<\cdots<x_q$ be the distinct zeros,
of multiplicities $\mu_i$.  Writing $\varphi=(t-x_i)^{\mu_i}\psi$ with
$\psi(x_i)\ne0$ shows that $\varphi'$ vanishes to order $\mu_i-1$ at $x_i$,
and Rolle's theorem gives a zero $y_i\in(x_i,x_{i+1})$ for $i<q$.  List
$\mu_1-1$ copies of $x_1$, then $y_1$, then $\mu_2-1$ copies of $x_2$, then
$y_2$, and so on: these are $m-1$ zeros of $\varphi'$ counted with
multiplicity, the $j$-th lies in $[\xi_j,\xi_{j+1}]$, and since
$-t^{z+1}/z$ has no zero in $I$ they are zeros of $T_zh$.  For
$(-\infty,0)$ apply this to $h(-t)$, using $(T_zh)(-t)=T_z\bigl(h(-t)\bigr)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Rows at real roots of both signs]\label{thm:bothsigns}
Let $F\in\mathbb C[x]$ be nonzero of degree $D$, and let
$a_1\ge\cdots\ge a_p>0$ and $c_1\ge\cdots\ge c_n>0$, repetitions allowed, with
$p+n\ge1$, be such that $\prod_{i}(x-a_i)\prod_i(x+c_i)$ divides $F$.  Let
$0<\rho<1$ with $\rho a_i>1$ and $\rho c_i>1$ for every $i$.  For
$1\le k\le\max(p,n)$ put
\[
  \Pi_k(\rho)=\prod_{i=k}^{p}(\rho a_i)\prod_{i=k}^{n}(\rho c_i),
\]
an empty product being $1$.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bothrowsbound}
  b_k(F)\ \ge\ |f_D|\,\frac{\Pi_k(\rho)-1}{(1-\rho)^{-k}-1}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
First let $F$ be real.  Since $k\le\max(p,n)\le p+n\le D$, there are at least
$k-1$ positions below $D$.  Let $z_1<\cdots<z_{k-1}$ be the backward distances
$D-j$ of $k-1$ positions $j<D$ carrying the $k-1$ largest nonleading
magnitudes; they are distinct positive integers, so $z_i\ge i$.  Let
$F^*(t)=t^DF(1/t)=\sum_{d=0}^Df_{D-d}t^d$ and
\[
  g=T_{z_{k-1}}\cdots T_{z_1}F^*,\qquad
  [t^d]g=f_{D-d}\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\Bigl(1-\frac d{z_i}\Bigr).
\]
So $g(0)=f_D\ne0$ and $[t^{z_i}]g=0$.  As in the proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:rowone}, $(x-\alpha)\mid F$ gives $(1-\alpha t)\mid F^*$,
so $F^*$ has the zeros $1/a_1\le\cdots\le1/a_p$ in $(0,\infty)$ and the zeros
$-1/c_i$, of absolute values $1/c_1\le\cdots\le1/c_n$, in $(-\infty,0)$, with
multiplicity.  Every intermediate polynomial $T_{z_s}\cdots T_{z_1}F^*$ has
constant term $f_D\ne0$, so Lemma~\ref{lem:rolleside} applies at each step;
after $s$ steps the $j$-th positive zero is at most the $(j+1)$-th positive
zero of the previous stage, hence at most $1/a_{j+s}$.  So $g$ has positive
zeros $\eta_j\le1/a_{j+k-1}$, $1\le j\le p-k+1$, and negative zeros
$\eta'_j$ with $|\eta'_j|\le1/c_{j+k-1}$, $1\le j\le n-k+1$, all of modulus
less than $\rho$.  Jensen's formula for $g$ on $|t|\le\rho$ gives
\[
  \log|f_D|+\log\Pi_k(\rho)\ \le\
  \log|f_D|+\sum_{g(\zeta)=0,\ |\zeta|<\rho}\log\frac\rho{|\zeta|}\ \le\
  \log\max_{|t|=\rho}|g(t)|,
\]
because $\log(\rho/|\eta_j|)\ge\log(\rho a_{j+k-1})$, likewise for the
$\eta'_j$, and the remaining terms are positive.  On the other side, for
$d\ge1$ either $[t^d]g=0$ or the position $D-d$ is not among the $k-1$ largest,
so $|f_{D-d}|\le b_k(F)$, while
$\prod_i|1-d/z_i|\le\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}(1+d/i)=\binom{d+k-1}{k-1}$.  Hence
\[
  \max_{|t|=\rho}|g(t)|\ \le\ |f_D|+b_k(F)\sum_{d\ge1}\binom{d+k-1}{k-1}\rho^d
  =|f_D|+b_k(F)\bigl((1-\rho)^{-k}-1\bigr),
\]
and the two displays give \eqref{eq:bothrowsbound}.  For complex $F$ apply the
real case to $\operatorname{Re}(F/f_D)$, as in Corollary~\ref{cor:complex}: it
is real of degree $D$ with leading coefficient $1$, divisible by the real
polynomial $\prod_i(x-a_i)\prod_i(x+c_i)$, and its coefficients are dominated
by those of $F/f_D$.
\end{proof}

The exclusions cost one root of each sign, and in the worst case the largest
one: $\Pi_k$ omits $a_1,\ldots,a_{k-1}$ and $c_1,\ldots,c_{k-1}$.  This is the
opposite of Theorem~\ref{thm:order}, which omits the smallest roots, so
\eqref{eq:bothrowsbound} is the right tool when the moduli are comparable, and
it is sharp there.

\begin{corollary}[Mass at real roots of both signs]\label{cor:bothsignsmass}
Let $F\in\mathbb C[x]$ be nonzero with $|f_D|\ge1$, divisible by
$\prod_{i=1}^L(x-r_i)$ with $r_i$ real, repetitions allowed, and $|r_i|\ge R\ge4$.
Then for $1\le k\le\lceil L/2\rceil$,
\[
  b_k(F)\ \ge\ |f_D|\,\frac{(R/2)^{L-2k+2}-1}{2^k-1},
\]
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bothmass}
  \Lambda(F)\ \ge\ \Bigl(\Bigl\lceil\frac L2\Bigr\rceil+1\Bigr)\log|f_D|
  +\Bigl\lfloor\frac{(L+1)^2}4\Bigr\rfloor\log\frac R2
  -\frac12\Bigl\lceil\frac L2\Bigr\rceil\Bigl(\Bigl\lceil\frac L2\Bigr\rceil+3\Bigr)\log2 .
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Let $p$ and $n$ count the positive and negative $r_i$; then
$\lceil L/2\rceil\le\max(p,n)$.  Take $\rho=\frac12$ in
Theorem~\ref{thm:bothsigns}: every $\rho|r_i|\ge R/2\ge2$, and
$\Pi_k(\tfrac12)\ge(R/2)^{(p-k+1)^++(n-k+1)^+}\ge(R/2)^{L-2k+2}$.  This is the row
bound.  For $k\le\lceil L/2\rceil$ the exponent $L-2k+2$ is at least $1$, so
$X=(R/2)^{L-2k+2}\ge2$, $X-1\ge X/2$, and
$\log b_k(F)\ge\log|f_D|+(L-2k+2)\log(R/2)-(k+1)\log2$.  The leading coefficient
and the $\lceil L/2\rceil$ largest nonleading magnitudes, all nonzero, sit at
distinct positions, so $\Lambda(F)\ge\log^+|f_D|+\sum_k\log^+b_k(F)$ is at
least the sum of these lower bounds; finally
$\sum_{k=1}^{\lceil L/2\rceil}(L-2k+2)=\lfloor(L+1)^2/4\rfloor$ and
$\sum_{k=1}^{\lceil L/2\rceil}(k+1)=\frac12\lceil L/2\rceil(\lceil L/2\rceil+3)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Sharpness of the constant $\frac14$]\label{prop:bothsignssharp}
Let $m\ge1$, $R\ge4$, $\lambda\ge1$ and $s_1,\ldots,s_m\in[R,\lambda R]$.  The
polynomial $P=\prod_{j=1}^m(x^2-s_j^2)$ is a monic multiple of the $L=2m$ real
roots $\pm s_j$, all of modulus at least $R$, and
\[
  \Lambda(P)\ \le\ \Bigl\lfloor\frac{(L+1)^2}4\Bigr\rfloor\log(\lambda R)+m^2\log2 .
\]
So the coefficient $\lfloor(L+1)^2/4\rfloor\sim\frac14L^2$ of $\log R$ in
\eqref{eq:bothmass} cannot be increased, while for roots of one sign
Corollary~\ref{cor:mass} gives $\frac{L(L+1)}2\log(R-1)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The nonleading coefficients of $P$ are $\pm e_j(s_1^2,\ldots,s_m^2)$,
$1\le j\le m$, each between $1$ and $\binom mj(\lambda R)^{2j}$.  So
$\Lambda(P)\le\sum_j\log\binom mj+m(m+1)\log(\lambda R)$, and
$m(m+1)=\lfloor(2m+1)^2/4\rfloor$, $\sum_j\log\binom mj\le m^2\log2$.
\end{proof}

At the bottom of the range the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:quadratic} goes
through for both signs.  If $|f_D|\ge1$ and all $|r_i|\ge2$, then
\eqref{eq:bothrowsbound} with $\rho=\frac9{10}$ gives
$b_k\ge((9/5)^{L-2k+2}-1)/(10^k-1)$; for $k\le L/16$ the exponent is at least
$\frac78L$, so for $L\ge16$
\[
  \log b_k\ \ge\ \tfrac78L\log\tfrac95-\tfrac1{16}L\log10
    +\log\bigl(1-(9/5)^{-14}\bigr)\ \ge\ \tfrac L3,
\]
with $\frac78\log\frac95=0.5143\ldots$ and $\frac1{16}\log10=0.1439\ldots$;
there are $\lfloor L/16\rfloor\ge L/32$ such rows, so $\Lambda(F)\ge L^2/96$.

\paragraph{Spare roots.}  Theorem~\ref{thm:bothsigns} drops, for each excluded
position, the largest root of each sign.  The rest of this subsection sets up
certificates that drop the smallest roots instead, as \eqref{eq:bothrows}
below asks, and proves the row $k=2$ up to a constant factor.  Write
$\|A\|_1$ for the sum of the absolute values of the coefficients of a
polynomial or power series $A$; it is submultiplicative, and for $|r|>1$
\[
  \Bigl\|\frac x{x-r}\Bigr\|_1=\Bigl\|\frac1{x-r}\Bigr\|_1=\frac1{|r|-1}.
\]
As in Lemma~\ref{lem:consecutive}, $h_n$ denotes the complete homogeneous
symmetric polynomial of degree $n$, with $h_n=0$ for $n<0$.

\begin{lemma}[Certificates with spare roots]\label{lem:sparecert}
Let $F\in\R[x]$ be a nonzero multiple of $P=\prod_{i=1}^L(x-r_i)$, with
$r_i$ real, $|r_i|>1$, repetitions allowed, and put $y_i=1/r_i$ and
$h_n=h_n(y_1,\ldots,y_L)$.  Let $1\le K\le L-1$ and $c=L-K$, let $W$ be a
set of positive integers, and let $\nu=\sum_{l=0}^K\nu_lx^l$ with $\nu_K=1$
satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sparecond}
  \sum_{l=0}^K\nu_l\,h_{z-c-l}=0\qquad(z\in W).
\end{equation}
Let $U$ be the set of positions $j<D$ with $D-j\in W$.  Then
\[
  \max_{j<D,\ j\notin U}|f_j|\ \ge\ |f_D|\,
  \frac{\prod_{i=1}^L(|r_i|-1)}{\|\nu\|_1}.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $\Delta(x)=x^c\nu(x)/P(x)=\sum_{d\ge0}\delta_dx^d$, a power series since
$P(0)\ne0$, with $\delta_0=0$ as $c\ge1$.  Since $x^c\nu$ is monic of degree
$L$, $1-\Delta=N/P$ with $\deg N\le L-1$.  If $F=PQ$ then $(1-\Delta)F=NQ$ has
degree at most $D-1$, so $[x^D]\bigl((1-\Delta)F\bigr)=0$, that is,
\[
  f_D=\sum_{d=1}^D\delta_d\,f_{D-d}.
\]
This is the identity of Lemma~\ref{lem:slack}, with repeated roots allowed.
From $P(x)=\prod_i(-r_i)\prod_i(1-y_ix)$ we get
$1/P(x)=(-1)^L(\prod_iy_i)\sum_{n\ge0}h_nx^n$, so
$\delta_z=(-1)^L(\prod_iy_i)\sum_l\nu_lh_{z-c-l}=0$ for $z\in W$.  Hence
$|f_D|\le\max_{j<D,j\notin U}|f_j|\cdot\|\Delta\|_1$.  Finally split the roots
into the $c$ roots of a factor $P_1$ and the $K$ roots of $P_2=P/P_1$; then
$\Delta=(x^c/P_1)\sum_l\nu_l\,x^l/P_2$, and for $0\le l\le K$ the series
$x^l/P_2$ is a product of $l$ factors $x/(x-r)$ and $K-l$ factors $1/(x-r)$,
$r$ running over the roots of $P_2$.  So
$\|\Delta\|_1\le\|\nu\|_1\prod_{i=1}^L(|r_i|-1)^{-1}$.
\end{proof}

With $\nu=x^K$, \eqref{eq:sparecond} holds for every $z\le L-1$, and $\Delta$
is the consecutive certificate of Lemma~\ref{lem:consecutive} on all $L$
nodes.  The point of the lemma is that the $K$ free coefficients of $\nu$ can
pay for excluded positions, while the price $\|\nu\|_1$ is compared with the
product over all roots.  For roots of one sign, $K=k-1$ free coefficients can
meet $k-1$ conditions; for both signs one needs $K=2k-2$, and this is exactly
Descartes' rule of signs.

\begin{proposition}[Two spare roots per excluded position]
\label{prop:sparedescartes}
Let $y_1,\ldots,y_L$ be nonzero reals, $k\ge1$, $2k-2\le K\le L-1$,
$c=L-K$, and let $W$ be a set of at most $k-1$ positive integers.  Then some
$\nu$ of degree $K$ with $\nu_K=1$ satisfies \eqref{eq:sparecond}.  If all
$y_i>0$, then $k-1\le K\le L-1$ suffices.  The bound $2k-2$ cannot be lowered:
for $L=2$, $(y_1,y_2)=(\frac12,-\frac12)$, $K=1$ and $W=\{4\}$ there is no
such $\nu$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Put $m_z=z-c$.  A row of \eqref{eq:sparecond} with $m_z<0$ is identically
zero, so we may assume $m_z\ge0$ for all $z\in W$.  The affine system
\eqref{eq:sparecond} in $\nu_0,\ldots,\nu_{K-1}$ is solvable unless some
$(\lambda_z)_{z\in W}$ has
\[
  \chi_l=\sum_{z\in W}\lambda_zh_{m_z-l}
\]
equal to $0$ for $0\le l<K$ and different from $0$ for $l=K$.  Suppose
$\chi_l=0$ for $l<K$.  Let $N=\max_zm_z$, $Y(x)=\prod_i(1-y_ix)$ and
$\Lambda(x)=\sum_z\lambda_zx^{N-m_z}$, a polynomial of degree at most $N$ with
at most $k-1$ terms.  The power series $\Lambda/Y=\sum_n\sigma_nx^n$ has
$\sigma_n=\sum_z\lambda_zh_{n-N+m_z}$, so $\chi_l=\sigma_{N-l}$; we must show
$\sigma_{N-K}=0$, which is trivial if $N<K$.  Let
$T=\sum_{n\le N}\sigma_nx^n$; by assumption $\deg T\le N-K$.  Since
$Y\cdot(\Lambda/Y-T)$ has only terms of degree above $N$,
\[
  A:=YT=\Lambda+x^{N+1}R,\qquad \deg R\le N-K+L-(N+1)=c-1 .
\]
If $T\ne0$ then $A\ne0$ (as $Y(0)=1$), and $A$ vanishes at the $L$ nonzero
reals $1/y_i$, with multiplicity.  By Descartes' rule, the number of positive
and of negative zeros of $A$ is at most the number of sign changes $V$ of the
coefficient sequence $(a_j)$ of $A$ and $V^-$ of $((-1)^ja_j)$.  A pair of
consecutive nonzero coefficients $a_d,a_{d'}$, $d<d'$, contributes at most
$\min(2,d'-d)$ to $V+V^-$, since for $d'=d+1$ exactly one of the two sequences
changes sign there.  The pairs inside the degrees $N+1,\ldots,N+c$ contribute
at most $c-1$ in total; every other pair has its left member among the at
most $k-1$ coefficients of $\Lambda$ and contributes at most $2$.  So
$L\le V+V^-\le c-1+2(k-1)\le L-1$, a contradiction.  Hence $T=0$ and
$\sigma_{N-K}=0$.  If all $y_i>0$, all zeros of $A$ are positive and
$L\le V\le c-1+(k-1)$, which fails once $K\ge k-1$.  In the example, $c=1$,
$m=3$, and \eqref{eq:sparecond} reads $h_2+\nu_0h_3=0$, while
$h_n(\frac12,-\frac12)=2^{-n}$ for even $n$ and $0$ for odd $n$.
\end{proof}

The proposition is qualitative.  For $k=2$ it can be made quantitative with
the following positivity of complete homogeneous polynomials of even degree,
which is due to Hunter~\cite{hunter}; we need it with explicit constants.

\begin{lemma}[Truncated exponentials]\label{lem:truncexp}
Let $e_n(u)=\sum_{i=0}^nu^i/i!$ and let $n\ge2$ be even.  For every real $u$,
\[
  e_n(u)>0,\qquad e_{n-2}(u)\le16\,e_n(u),\qquad |e_{n-1}(u)|\le6\,e_n(u).
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For $u\ge0$ we have $0<e_{n-2}(u)\le e_{n-1}(u)\le e_n(u)$.  Let $u=-a$ with
$a>0$.  As $n$ is even,
$\frac d{da}\bigl(e^ae_n(-a)\bigr)=e^a\bigl(e_n(-a)-e_{n-1}(-a)\bigr)=e^aa^n/n!$,
so
\[
  e_n(-a)=e^{-a}+\frac1{n!}\int_0^a(a-v)^ne^{-v}\,dv
  \ \ge\ e^{-a}+\frac{a^{n+1}}{n!}\int_0^1(1-w)^{n+a}\,dw
  =e^{-a}+\frac{a^{n+1}}{(n+a+1)\,n!},
\]
using $e^{-aw}\ge(1-w)^a$.  In particular $e_n(-a)>0$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:truncexp1}
  \frac{a^n}{n!}\le\Bigl(1+\frac{n+1}a\Bigr)e_n(-a).
\end{equation}
Let $\beta=0.278$, so that $\beta e^{1+\beta}=0.9978\ldots<1$.  Since
$a^je^a$ increases in $a>0$ and $n!\ge\sqrt{2\pi n}\,(n/e)^n$, for
$0<a\le\beta n$
\begin{equation}\label{eq:truncexp2}
  \frac{a^n}{n!}\le e^{-a}\,\frac{(\beta e^{1+\beta})^n}{\sqrt{2\pi n}}\le e^{-a},
  \qquad
  \frac{a^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}\le e^{-a}\,\frac{(\beta n)^ne^{\beta n}}{\beta\,n!}
  \le\frac{e^{-a}}{\beta\sqrt{2\pi n}} .
\end{equation}

\emph{The odd index.}  $e_{n-1}(-a)=e_n(-a)-a^n/n!$, so
$|e_{n-1}(-a)|\le\max\bigl(e_n(-a),a^n/n!\bigr)$.  If $a\le\beta n$,
\eqref{eq:truncexp2} gives $a^n/n!\le e_n(-a)$.  If $a>\beta n$ and $n\ge4$,
\eqref{eq:truncexp1} gives $a^n/n!\le(1+\frac5{4\beta})e_n(-a)\le5.5\,e_n(-a)$.
If $n=2$, $a^2/2\le(a-1)^2+1=2e_2(-a)$.

\emph{The index $n-2$.}  $e_{n-2}(-a)=e_n(-a)+\frac{a^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}(1-\frac an)$.
If $a\ge n$ this is at most $e_n(-a)$.  If $n=2$, $e_0=1\le2e_2(-a)$ because
$e_2(-a)=\frac12\bigl((a-1)^2+1\bigr)$.  Let $n\ge4$ and $a<n$.  If
$a\le\beta n$, \eqref{eq:truncexp2} bounds the extra term by
$e^{-a}/(\beta\sqrt{8\pi})\le e_n(-a)$, as $\beta\sqrt{8\pi}=1.39\ldots$.  If
$\beta n<a<n$, write $a=\alpha n$; by \eqref{eq:truncexp1} the extra term is
\[
  \frac{a^n}{n!}\cdot\frac{n-a}a\le\frac{(n+a+1)(n-a)}{a^2}\,e_n(-a)
  \le\Bigl(\frac5{4\alpha^2}-\frac1{4\alpha}-1\Bigr)e_n(-a)
  \le14.3\,e_n(-a),
\]
since $1/n\le\frac14$ and the bracket decreases in $\alpha\in(0,1)$.  So
$e_{n-2}(-a)\le15.3\,e_n(-a)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Even complete homogeneous polynomials]\label{lem:evenh}
Let $y_1,\ldots,y_L$ be real, not all $0$, let $T=\max_i|y_i|$ and
$h_n=h_n(y_1,\ldots,y_L)$.  For every even $n\ge2$,
\[
  h_n>0,\qquad T^2h_{n-2}\le16\,h_n,\qquad T\,|h_{n-1}|\le6\,h_n .
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $X_1,\ldots,X_L$ be independent exponential random variables of mean
$1$.  Expanding and using $\mathbb E X^a=a!$,
$\mathbb E\bigl[(\sum_iy_iX_i)^j\bigr]=j!\,h_j$ for every $j\ge0$.  Replacing
$y$ by $-y$ multiplies $h_j$ by $(-1)^j$ and changes none of the claims, so we
may assume $y_1=T>0$.  For real $r$,
$\mathbb E(TX_1+r)^j=\sum_i\binom jiT^ii!\,r^{j-i}=j!\,T^je_j(r/T)$.
Conditioning on $R=\sum_{i\ge2}y_iX_i$, which is independent of $X_1$, gives
$h_j=\mathbb E\bigl[T^je_j(R/T)\bigr]$ for all $j$, and
Lemma~\ref{lem:truncexp}, applied pointwise at $u=R/T$, gives the three
claims.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The second row at real roots of both signs]\label{thm:secondrow}
Let $F\in\mathbb C[x]$ be a nonzero multiple of $\prod_{i=1}^L(x-r_i)$,
$L\ge3$, with $r_i$ real, $|r_i|\ge2$, repetitions allowed, and moduli
$\rho_1\le\cdots\le\rho_L$.  Then
\[
  b_2(F)\ \ge\ |f_D|\,\frac{(\rho_1-1)(\rho_2-1)}{1+16\rho_1^2}
  \prod_{i=3}^L(\rho_i-1)
  \ \ge\ \frac{|f_D|}{65}\prod_{i=3}^L(\rho_i-1).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For complex $F$ apply the real case to $\operatorname{Re}(F/f_D)$, as in the
proof of Corollary~\ref{cor:complex}; so let $F$ be real.  As in the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:order}, it suffices to show that for every position $u<D$
some $j<D$, $j\ne u$, has $|f_j|$ at least the first bound.  Put $z=D-u$,
$K=2$, $c=L-2$, $m=z-c$, $y_i=1/r_i$ and $h_n=h_n(y_1,\ldots,y_L)$, so that
$T=\max_i|y_i|=1/\rho_1$.  Define
\[
  \nu=\begin{cases}
    x^2 & m\le1,\\[2pt]
    x^2-h_{m-2}/h_m & m\ge2\text{ even},\\[2pt]
    x^2-(h_{m-2}/h_{m-1})\,x & m\ge3\text{ odd}.
  \end{cases}
\]
The denominators are positive by Lemma~\ref{lem:evenh}, and in each case
$\sum_l\nu_lh_{m-l}=0$, which is \eqref{eq:sparecond} for $W=\{z\}$ (for
$m\le1$ because $h_{m-2}=0$).  By Lemma~\ref{lem:evenh},
$h_{m-2}/h_m\le16\rho_1^2$ for even $m$ and $|h_{m-2}|/h_{m-1}\le6\rho_1$ for
odd $m$, so $\|\nu\|_1\le1+16\rho_1^2$, as $\rho_1\ge2$.
Lemma~\ref{lem:sparecert} with $U=\{u\}$ gives the first bound.  For the
second, $(\rho_1-1)(\rho_2-1)\ge(\rho_1-1)^2$, and $(\rho-1)^2/(1+16\rho^2)$
increases for $\rho\ge1$, with value $1/65$ at $\rho=2$.
\end{proof}

The spare roots are the two of smallest modulus, as in \eqref{eq:bothrows},
and the proof uses only the largest node $1/\rho_1$.  Two limits of the method
are worth recording.  First, Lemma~\ref{lem:sparecert} alone cannot give the
constant $1$: for one excluded distance the least $\|\nu\|_1$ is
$1+|h_{m-2}|/\max(|h_m|,|h_{m-1}|)$, and for the roots $2,-2,R$ and even $m$
this tends to $5$ as $R\to\infty$, while $(\rho_1-1)(\rho_2-1)=1$.
The loss is in the estimate $\|\Delta\|_1\le\|\nu\|_1\prod_i(|r_i|-1)^{-1}$
and not in the row: at these roots the row holds with the constant $1$
(Theorem~\ref{thm:secondrowsharp} below).  Second,
for $k\ge3$ Lemma~\ref{lem:sparecert} and
Proposition~\ref{prop:sparedescartes} reduce \eqref{eq:bothrows}, up to a
factor $(2\kappa)^{2k-2}$, to the bound
\begin{equation}\label{eq:nuconj}
  N_{2k-2}(y;W)\ \le\ \kappa^{2k-2}\prod_{j=1}^{2k-2}\rho_j
  \qquad(|W|\le k-1)
\end{equation}
for the least $\|\nu\|_1=:N_{2k-2}(y;W)$ over the $\nu$ of
Proposition~\ref{prop:sparedescartes}, the spare roots being those of
moduli $\rho_1,\ldots,\rho_{2k-2}$; indeed
$\prod_{j\le2k-2}(\rho_j-1)\ge2^{2-2k}\prod_{j\le2k-2}\rho_j$.
Theorem~\ref{thm:secondrow} is \eqref{eq:nuconj} for $k=2$ with
$\kappa^2=\frac{65}4$.  By linear programming duality,
$N_{2k-2}(y;W)-1$ is the largest $|\chi_{2k-2}|$ over the $\lambda$ with
$|\chi_l|\le1$ for $l<2k-2$, in the notation of the proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:sparedescartes}: \eqref{eq:nuconj} says that a
combination of $k-1$ shifted copies of $(h_n)$ cannot be small at $2k-2$
consecutive places and large at the next one, which is a quantitative form of
the sign count in that proof.  For $k=2$ the pivot was an $h$ of even index, chosen by
the parity of $m$; for $k\ge3$ the determinants of the square subsystems of
\eqref{eq:sparecond} are skew Schur functions in variables of both signs,
which have no sign, and we have no substitute for Lemma~\ref{lem:evenh}.  We computed $N_{2k-2}(y;W)$ exactly, by enumerating the
supports of the basic solutions of the linear program, on random rational
root sets with near-pairs $r,-r(1+\eta)$ and moduli between $2$ and $16$, and
at the worst configurations found by a floating-point search near modulus
$2$.  The largest values of $N_{2k-2}(y;W)/\prod_{j\le2k-2}\rho_j$ were
$\frac54$ for $k=2$ (the limit above), $1.49$ for $k=3$ and $0.91$ for $k=4$,
so \eqref{eq:nuconj} with $\kappa=1.12$ is consistent with all the data.

\paragraph{Mixing certificates.}  The estimate
$\|\Delta\|_1\le\|\nu\|_1\prod_i(|r_i|-1)^{-1}$ at the end of the proof of
Lemma~\ref{lem:sparecert} is where the constant is lost.  Certificates of
sub-multisets of the roots can instead be averaged, and averaging does not
increase the norm.  For a nonempty sub-multiset $A$ of the roots write
$P_A=\prod_{i\in A}(x-r_i)$ and
\[
  C_A=\frac{x^{|A|}}{P_A}=\sum_{d\ge0}\gamma^A_dx^d,\qquad
  \gamma^A_d=(-1)^{|A|}\Bigl(\prod_{i\in A}y_i\Bigr)h_{d-|A|}(A),
\]
the consecutive certificate of Lemma~\ref{lem:consecutive} on the nodes of
$A$; here $h_n(A)$ is the complete homogeneous polynomial in the $y_i$,
$i\in A$, and the formula for $\gamma^A_d$ follows from
$1/P_A=(-1)^{|A|}(\prod_{i\in A}y_i)\sum_nh_n(A)x^n$.

\begin{lemma}[Mixing certificates]\label{lem:mixcert}
Let $F\in\R[x]$ be a nonzero multiple of $P=\prod_{i=1}^L(x-r_i)$, with $r_i$
real, $|r_i|>1$, repetitions allowed.  Let $A_1,\ldots,A_p$ be nonempty
sub-multisets of the roots, $\theta_1,\ldots,\theta_p\ge0$ with
$\sum_q\theta_q=1$, and let $W$ be a set of positive integers with
$\sum_q\theta_q\gamma^{A_q}_z=0$ for $z\in W$.  If $U$ is the set of positions
$j<D$ with $D-j\in W$, then
\[
  \max_{j<D,\ j\notin U}|f_j|\ \ge\ |f_D|\,\min_q\prod_{i\in A_q}(|r_i|-1).
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since $P_A$ is monic, $1-C_A=(P_A-x^{|A|})/P_A$ with a numerator of degree
below $|A|$; as $P_A$ divides $F$, the product $(1-C_A)F$ is a polynomial of
degree below $D$.  So $[x^D]\bigl((1-C_A)F\bigr)=0$, that is,
$f_D=\sum_{d\ge1}\gamma^A_df_{D-d}$, because $\gamma^A_0=0$.  Averaging these
identities with the weights $\theta_q$ gives $f_D=\sum_{d\ge1}\delta_df_{D-d}$
with $\delta=\sum_q\theta_q\gamma^{A_q}$, and $\delta_z=0$ for $z\in W$.  Hence
$|f_D|\le\max_{j<D,j\notin U}|f_j|\cdot\sum_q\theta_q\|C_{A_q}\|_1$, and
$\|C_A\|_1\le\prod_{i\in A}\|x/(x-r_i)\|_1=\prod_{i\in A}(|r_i|-1)^{-1}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[The second row, sharper]\label{thm:secondrowsharp}
Let $F\in\mathbb C[x]$ be a nonzero multiple of $\prod_{i=1}^L(x-r_i)$, $L\ge3$,
with $r_i$ real, $|r_i|>1$, repetitions allowed.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] If $r_a\ge2$ and $r_b\le-2$ for two indices $a\ne b$, then
\[
  b_2(F)\ \ge\ |f_D|\prod_{i\ne a,b}(|r_i|-1).
\]
In particular, if all $|r_i|\ge2$ and two roots of opposite signs have the
moduli $\rho_1,\rho_2$, then \eqref{eq:bothrows} holds for $k=2$.
\item[(ii)] If all $|r_i|\ge2$ and $\rho_1\le\cdots\le\rho_L$ are the moduli,
then
\[
  b_2(F)\ \ge\ |f_D|\min\Bigl(\frac{\rho_2-1}{1+6\rho_2},
  \frac{(\rho_1-1)(\rho_2-1)}{1+6\rho_1}\Bigr)\prod_{i=3}^L(\rho_i-1)
  \ \ge\ \frac{|f_D|}{13}\prod_{i=3}^L(\rho_i-1).
\]
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For complex $F$ apply the real case to $\operatorname{Re}(F/f_D)$, as in the
proof of Corollary~\ref{cor:complex}; so let $F$ be real, and let $Y$ denote
the multiset of all roots.  As in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:order}, it
suffices to show that for every position $u<D$ some $j<D$, $j\ne u$, has
$|f_j|$ at least the claimed bound.  Put $z=D-u\ge1$ and $n=z-L$.

(i) Let $B=Y\setminus\{a,b\}$, which is nonempty as $L\ge3$.  Every $A$ with
$B\subseteq A\subseteq Y$ has
$\prod_{i\in A}(|r_i|-1)\ge\prod_{i\in B}(|r_i|-1)$, because
$|r_a|-1\ge1$ and $|r_b|-1\ge1$; so by Lemma~\ref{lem:mixcert} it suffices to
average certificates $C_A$ of such $A$ so that the coefficient of $x^z$
vanishes.  If $n<0$ then $\gamma^Y_z=0$, and $C_Y$ alone will do.  If $n\ge0$
is even, then
\[
  \gamma^Y_z=(-1)^L\Bigl(\prod_{i\in Y}y_i\Bigr)h_n(Y),\qquad
  \gamma^B_z=(-1)^L\Bigl(\prod_{i\in B}y_i\Bigr)h_{n+2}(B),
\]
both complete homogeneous polynomials are positive by
Lemma~\ref{lem:evenh} (or because $h_0=1$), and the two coefficients have
opposite signs because $y_ay_b<0$.  So
$\theta\gamma^Y_z+(1-\theta)\gamma^B_z=0$ for some $\theta\in[0,1]$.  If $n$
is odd, then $n+1\ge2$ is even,
$\gamma^{Y\setminus\{a\}}_z=(-1)^{L-1}(\prod_{i\ne a}y_i)h_{n+1}(Y\setminus\{a\})$
and likewise for $b$; both $h_{n+1}$ are positive, and the two coefficients
have opposite signs because $y_b/y_a<0$.  Again a convex combination has
vanishing coefficient of $x^z$.

(ii) Put $m=z-L+2$.  If $m\le1$, then $\gamma^Y_z=0$ and $C_Y$ gives
$|f_j|\ge|f_D|\prod_i(\rho_i-1)$.  If $m\ge3$ is odd, the certificate
$\nu=x^2-(h_{m-2}/h_{m-1})x$ of the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:secondrow} has
$\|\nu\|_1\le1+6\rho_1$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:evenh}, and Lemma~\ref{lem:sparecert}
gives the bound $(\rho_1-1)(\rho_2-1)/(1+6\rho_1)$ times
$\prod_{i\ge3}(\rho_i-1)$.  Let $m\ge2$ be even, let $r_a$ be a root of modulus
$\rho_1$ and $A=Y\setminus\{a\}$; the largest node of $A$ has modulus
$1/\rho_2$.  Put $\beta=-h_{m-1}(A)/h_m(A)$, so that $|\beta|\le6\rho_2$ by
Lemma~\ref{lem:evenh}, and
\[
  \Delta=\frac{x^{L-2}(x+\beta)}{P_A}=\sum_{d\ge0}\delta_dx^d,\qquad
  \delta_z=(-1)^{L-1}\Bigl(\prod_{i\in A}y_i\Bigr)
  \bigl(h_{m-1}(A)+\beta h_m(A)\bigr)=0 .
\]
As $P_A$ is monic of degree $L-1$ and $L-2\ge1$, the argument of
Lemma~\ref{lem:mixcert} gives $f_D=\sum_{d\ge1}\delta_df_{D-d}$.  Writing
$\Delta=\bigl(x^{L-2}/P_{A\setminus\{b\}}\bigr)\cdot(x+\beta)/(x-r_b)$ with
$|r_b|=\rho_2$, and using $\|(x+\beta)/(x-r)\|_1\le(1+|\beta|)/(|r|-1)$, we
get $\|\Delta\|_1\le(1+6\rho_2)(\rho_2-1)^{-1}\prod_{i\ge3}(\rho_i-1)^{-1}$,
which is the first term of the minimum.  Finally $(1+6\rho)/(\rho-1)$
decreases for $\rho>1$, and
$(\rho_1-1)(\rho_2-1)/(1+6\rho_1)\ge(\rho_1-1)^2/(1+6\rho_1)$, which increases;
both give $\frac1{13}$ at $\rho=2$.
\end{proof}

Part (i) is sharp in the sense that it has the constant $1$ of
\eqref{eq:bothrows}, although it uses no quantitative form of
Lemma~\ref{lem:evenh}, only the positivity of $h_{2n}$.  At the roots
$2,-2,R$, where the best $\nu$ in Lemma~\ref{lem:sparecert} costs a factor
tending to $5$, part (i) gives $b_2\ge|f_D|(R-1)$.  The same averaging of the
certificates $C_A$ can fail when the two roots of smallest modulus have the same
sign and the odd complete homogeneous polynomial that appears in the proof has
the wrong sign.  Part (ii) is then the fallback, and it improves the factor
$65$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:secondrow} to $13$ by using, for even $m$, one spare
root instead of two.

\paragraph{Rows $k\ge3$.}  Two remarks narrow the open bound
\eqref{eq:nuconj}.  First, it has an equivalent form without the factor
$\prod_j\rho_j$.  In the notation of the proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:sparedescartes}, let $S$ be the multiset of the $2k-2$
nodes of largest modulus, $Y'$ the remaining nodes and $Y=Y'\cup S$, and put
$\chi_l=\sum_{z\in W}\lambda_zh_{m_z-l}(Y)$ and
$\omega=\sum_{z\in W}\lambda_zh_{m_z}(Y')$.  Multiplying
$\sum_nh_n(Y)x^n$ by $\prod_{s\in S}(1-sx)$ gives $\sum_nh_n(Y')x^n$, so
$\omega=\sum_{i=0}^{2k-2}(-1)^ie_i(S)\chi_i$, where
$\sum_i|e_i(S)|\le\prod_{s\in S}(1+|s|)\le(\frac32)^{2k-2}$ and
$|e_{2k-2}(S)|=\prod_{j\le2k-2}\rho_j^{-1}$.  With the linear programming
description of $N_{2k-2}(y;W)-1$ as the largest $|\chi_{2k-2}|$ subject to
$|\chi_l|\le1$ for $l<2k-2$, it follows that \eqref{eq:nuconj} holds for some
$\kappa$ if and only if, for some constant $B$, all node sets, all $W$ and all $\lambda$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:nucore}
  \Bigl|\sum_{z\in W}\lambda_zh_{m_z}(Y')\Bigr|\ \le\ B^{2k-2}
  \max_{0\le l<2k-2}\Bigl|\sum_{z\in W}\lambda_zh_{m_z-l}(Y)\Bigr| .
\end{equation}
Precisely, \eqref{eq:nuconj} gives \eqref{eq:nucore} with
$B^{2k-2}=\kappa^{2k-2}+(\frac32)^{2k-2}$, and \eqref{eq:nucore} gives
\eqref{eq:nuconj} with $\kappa^{2k-2}=1+B^{2k-2}+(\frac32)^{2k-2}$.  In the
probabilistic language of the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:evenh},
\eqref{eq:nucore} compares $\mathbb E\,g(Z')$ with
$\mathbb E\,g^{(l)}(Z'+\sum_{s\in S}sX_s)$, $l<2k-2$, for the
$(k-1)$-term polynomial $g(u)=\sum_z\lambda_zu^{m_z}/m_z!$ and
$Z'=\sum_{y\in Y'}yX_y$.  Second, the remark after Corollary~\ref{cor:conjcross} shows that the
applications only need
\eqref{eq:nuconj} with $\kappa^{2k-2}$ replaced by $C^L$.

Averaging, as in part (i) of Theorem~\ref{thm:secondrowsharp}, looks like the
right mechanism for all rows when the signs are balanced.  We conjecture the
following.  Let $F$ be a nonzero real multiple of $\prod_{i=1}^L(x-r_i)$ with $r_i$ real,
$|r_i|\ge2$, let $2\le k\le\lceil L/2\rceil$, and suppose that $2k-2$ roots of
moduli $\rho_1,\ldots,\rho_{2k-2}$ can be chosen with exactly $k-1$ of them
positive.  Then for every set $W$ of
$k-1$ positive integers some convex combination of the certificates $C_A$,
where $A$ runs over the sub-multisets that contain all the other $L-2k+2$
roots (each has $\|C_A\|_1\le\prod_{i\ge2k-1}(\rho_i-1)^{-1}$), has vanishing coefficients at $W$.  By Lemma~\ref{lem:mixcert}, this
would give \eqref{eq:bothrows} for the row $k$, with the constant $1$.
For $k=2$ this is part (i) of Theorem~\ref{thm:secondrowsharp}.  We tested it
with a floating-point linear program, on random root sets with the $2k-2$
smallest moduli between $2$ and $6$ and the others up to about $190$, exact
pairs $\pm s$ and near-pairs included, and random $W\subset[1,80]$.  The combination
existed in all $550$ balanced instances with $k=3$, all $270$ with $k=4$ and
all $40$ with $k=5$.  For some unbalanced splits it does not exist.  With the
roots $2.319,-2.351,2.519,2.569,-2.595,-2.646$ and $W=\{14,26\}$ ($k=3$,
three positive spare roots), an exact rational vector certifies, by Farkas'
lemma, that no convex combination of the sixteen certificates $C_A$ vanishes
on $W$.  The row itself is not in doubt there: in floating point, the least
$\|x^c\nu/P\|_1$ over the admissible $\nu$ of Lemma~\ref{lem:sparecert} is about
$0.06\prod_{i\ge5}(\rho_i-1)^{-1}$.  Such unbalanced cases, and the case of one sign
among the smallest roots, therefore need a quantitative argument like
part (ii).  The conjecture would not settle \eqref{eq:bothrows} by itself.

\subsection{Gaussian-integer roots}\label{sec:gaussian}

Proposition~\ref{prop:noangle} rules out bounds in terms of moduli and
angles.  For roots in $\mathbb Z[i]$ and integer multiples there is one more
piece of information: an integer polynomial takes Gaussian-integer values at
them, and a nonzero Gaussian integer has modulus at least one.  This
subsection shows what that buys.  It does not give the rows $k\ge2$ at
Gaussian pairs, but it reduces the mass problem to multiples of bounded
degree, which makes it a finite computation for each root set
(Corollary~\ref{cor:gaussfinite}), and it certifies the imaginary-pair
charge $\sum_jj\log(c_j^2-1)$ at explicit root sets with $a_j\ne0$
(Proposition~\ref{prop:gaussinst}).  Proposition~\ref{prop:gaussnotransfer}
records why the exact reductions of this section cannot be used at such
roots.  Theorem~\ref{thm:thingauss} gives the mass bound for crowded moduli when
the roots lie in a thin sector, which covers register roots $a\pm p^bi$
with $|a|$ bounded (Corollary~\ref{cor:thinregister}).

Throughout, $\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_K\in\mathbb Z[i]$ are distinct, with
$\alpha_j=a_j+ic_j$ and $c_j\ge1$; put $\rho_j=|\alpha_j|$,
$\rho_{\min}=\min_j\rho_j$ and
\[
  P=\prod_{j=1}^K(x-\alpha_j)(x-\bar\alpha_j)
   =\prod_{j=1}^K\bigl(x^2-2a_jx+a_j^2+c_j^2\bigr)\in\mathbb Z[x],
\]
a monic polynomial with $2K$ distinct roots and $P(0)\ne0$.  For
$F=\sum_{i=0}^Df_ix^i$ and $0\le m\le D+1$ write
\[
  R_m=\sum_{i<m}f_ix^i,\qquad G_m=\sum_{i\ge m}f_ix^{i-m},\qquad
  F=R_m+x^mG_m .
\]
We call $m\in\{1,\ldots,D\}$ a \emph{split} of $F$.  A split is \emph{clean
at} $\alpha$ if $G_m(\alpha)=0$, and \emph{$P$-clean} if it is clean at every
$\alpha_j$.  A polynomial $B\in\mathbb Z[x]$ is \emph{$P$-irreducible} if
$B(0)\ne0$ and no split of $B$ is $P$-clean.

\begin{lemma}[Gaussian carries]\label{lem:gausscarry}
Let $F\in\mathbb Z[x]$ have degree $D$, let $\alpha\in\mathbb Z[i]$ with
$F(\alpha)=0$, $\rho=|\alpha|$, and let $1\le m\le D$.  Then
$G_m(\alpha)\in\mathbb Z[i]$ and $R_m(\alpha)=-\alpha^mG_m(\alpha)$.  If the
split $m$ is not clean at $\alpha$, then $|R_m(\alpha)|\ge\rho^m$, and if
moreover $\rho>2$, some $i<m$ has
\[
  |f_i|\ \ge\ (\rho/2)^{m-i}.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
$G_m$ has integer coefficients, so $G_m(\alpha)\in\mathbb Z[i]$, and
$0=F(\alpha)=R_m(\alpha)+\alpha^mG_m(\alpha)$.  If $G_m(\alpha)\ne0$ it is a
nonzero Gaussian integer, so $|G_m(\alpha)|\ge1$ and
$|R_m(\alpha)|=\rho^m|G_m(\alpha)|\ge\rho^m$.  If every $i<m$ had
$|f_i|<(\rho/2)^{m-i}$, then
$|R_m(\alpha)|\le\sum_{i<m}|f_i|\rho^i<\rho^m\sum_{i<m}2^{i-m}<\rho^m$, a
contradiction.
\end{proof}

The lemma is the Gaussian form of the gap principle for lacunary
polynomials~\cite{lenstra-lacunary}: across a run of small coefficients a
root of $F$ must be a root of both parts.  Here it is used at every split,
not only at long gaps.

\begin{lemma}[Blocks]\label{lem:gaussblocks}
Every nonzero $F\in\mathbb Z[x]$ divisible by $P$ can be written
\[
  F=\sum_{s=1}^rx^{e_s}B_s,\qquad e_s+\deg B_s<e_{s+1},
\]
with every $B_s\in\mathbb Z[x]$ $P$-irreducible and divisible by $P$.
Consequently $\Lambda(F)=\sum_s\Lambda(B_s)$, $|\operatorname{lead}B_s|\ge1$,
and $b_k(F)\ge b_k(B_s)$ for every $s$ and $k$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write $F=x^eF_0$ with $F_0(0)\ne0$; since $P(0)\ne0$, $P\mid F_0$.  Induct
on $\deg F_0$.  If no split of $F_0$ is $P$-clean, $F_0$ itself is the only
block.  Otherwise let $m$ be a $P$-clean split: $G_m(\alpha_j)=0$ for all
$j$, hence $R_m(\alpha_j)=F_0(\alpha_j)-\alpha_j^mG_m(\alpha_j)=0$, and by
reality of the coefficients also $R_m(\bar\alpha_j)=G_m(\bar\alpha_j)=0$.
Since $P$ is monic in $\mathbb Z[x]$ with $2K$ distinct roots, division with
remainder in $\mathbb Z[x]$ shows $P\mid R_m$ and $P\mid G_m$ in
$\mathbb Z[x]$.  Both are nonzero ($R_m(0)=F_0(0)$, and $G_m$ has the leading
coefficient of $F_0$) and of smaller degree than $F_0$; $R_m(0)\ne0$, and
$G_m$ is handled after removing a power of $x$.  Their supports lie in
$[0,m-1]$ and $[m,\deg F_0]$, so the decompositions given by induction
concatenate.  The supports of the $x^{e_s}B_s$ are disjoint and cover that of
$F$, which gives the additivity of $\Lambda$ and the inclusion of the
nonleading magnitudes of each $B_s$ among those of $F$ (the leading
coefficient of $B_s$, $s<r$, is a nonleading coefficient of $F$).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Degree charging]\label{thm:gausscharge}
Assume $\rho_{\min}>2$, and let $B\in\mathbb Z[x]$ be $P$-irreducible and
divisible by $P$, of degree $d$.  Then
\[
  \sum_{i<d}\log^+|b_i|\ \ge\ d\log\frac{\rho_{\min}}2,
  \qquad\text{so}\qquad
  \Lambda(B)\ \ge\ \log|b_d|+d\log\frac{\rho_{\min}}2 .
\]
Hence every nonzero $F\in\mathbb Z[x]$ divisible by $P$ has
$\Lambda(F)\ge\sum_s(\deg B_s)\log(\rho_{\min}/2)$ over its blocks.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Every split $m\in\{1,\ldots,d\}$ of $B$ is unclean at some $\alpha_j$, with
$\rho_j\ge\rho_{\min}$, so by Lemma~\ref{lem:gausscarry} there is $i<m$ with
$|b_i|\ge(\rho_{\min}/2)^{m-i}$.  For a fixed $i$, the splits charged to it
this way have distinct $m-i\ge1$ with
$(m-i)\log(\rho_{\min}/2)\le\log|b_i|$, so there are at most
$\log^+|b_i|/\log(\rho_{\min}/2)$ of them.  All $d$ splits are charged, which
is the first inequality.  The rest follows from $\log^+|b_d|\ge\log|b_d|$ and
Lemma~\ref{lem:gaussblocks}.
\end{proof}

Compare Proposition~\ref{prop:noangle}: $x^{2M}+\rho^{2M}$ pays
$\Lambda=2M\log\rho$ for its degree $2M$, which the theorem allows; what the
theorem forbids, at Gaussian roots, is a block of large degree and small
mass.  So a cheap multiple of a Gaussian pair product, if one exists, has
small degree, and this is what makes the mass problem finite.

\begin{corollary}[Finite reduction of the mass problem]\label{cor:gaussfinite}
Assume $\rho_{\min}>2$ and let $X>0$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item If every $P$-irreducible $B\in\mathbb Z[x]$ divisible by $P$ with
$\deg B<X/\log(\rho_{\min}/2)$ has $\Lambda(B)\ge X$, then every nonzero
$F\in\mathbb Z[x]$ divisible by $P$ has $\Lambda(F)\ge X$.
\item The infimum of $\Lambda(F)$ over nonzero $F\in\mathbb Z[x]$ divisible by
$P$ is a minimum, attained by a $P$-irreducible multiple of degree at most
$\Lambda(P)/\log(\rho_{\min}/2)$ whose coefficients have modulus at most
$e^{\Lambda(P)}$; in particular it is computable.
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
1.  Let $B$ be any block of $F$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:gaussblocks}).  If
$\deg B<X/\log(\rho_{\min}/2)$ then $\Lambda(F)\ge\Lambda(B)\ge X$ by
hypothesis; otherwise Theorem~\ref{thm:gausscharge} gives
$\Lambda(F)\ge\Lambda(B)\ge\deg B\cdot\log(\rho_{\min}/2)\ge X$.

2.  $P$ itself competes, so the infimum is at most $\Lambda(P)$.  If
$\Lambda(F)\le\Lambda(P)$, any block $B$ of $F$ has $\Lambda(B)\le\Lambda(F)$,
degree at most $\Lambda(P)/\log(\rho_{\min}/2)$ by
Theorem~\ref{thm:gausscharge}, and $\log^+|b_i|\le\Lambda(P)$ for every $i$.
There are finitely many such integer polynomials.
\end{proof}

For the certificates below, rows at one degree control all smaller degrees.

\begin{lemma}[Rows are monotone in the degree]\label{lem:rowmono}
For $d\ge2K$ and $k\ge1$ let $\beta_k(d)$ be the infimum of $b_k(G)$ over
monic $G\in\R[x]$ of degree $d$ divisible by $P$.  Then
$\beta_k(d+1)\le\beta_k(d)$, and every $B\in\mathbb Z[x]$ divisible by $P$
with $\deg B\le d$ has $b_k(B)\ge|\operatorname{lead}B|\,\beta_k(d)\ge\beta_k(d)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
$xG$ has the same nonzero coefficients as $G$.  For $B$, the polynomial
$B/\operatorname{lead}B$ is a monic real multiple of $P$ of degree $\deg B$, and
$b_k$ scales by $|\operatorname{lead}B|\ge1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Certified instances]\label{prop:gaussinst}
Every nonzero $F\in\mathbb Z[x]$ divisible by the product of the displayed
Gaussian pairs satisfies the displayed bound, which exceeds the charge
$\sum_jj\log(c_{(j)}^2-1)$ that Corollary~\ref{cor:imag} makes for the
purely imaginary pairs $\pm ic_j$ with the same imaginary parts:
\[
\begin{array}{lll}
\text{pairs} & \Lambda(F)\ \ge & \sum_jj\log(c_{(j)}^2-1)\\[2pt]
1\pm5i,\ -2\pm7i & 11.09 & 10.92\\
1\pm5i,\ -2\pm7i,\ 3\pm11i & 26.20 & 25.28\\
1\pm97i,\ -2\pm101i,\ 3\pm103i & 58.22 & 55.42\\
1\pm1009i,\ -2\pm1013i,\ 3\pm1019i,\ -4\pm1021i & 139.8 & 138.5
\end{array}
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $X$ be the right-hand charge and $d_0$ the least integer with
$d_0\log(\rho_{\min}/2)\ge X$, that is, with
$(\rho_{\min}^2/4)^{d_0}\ge\prod_j(c_{(j)}^2-1)^{2j}$, an integer comparison:
$d_0=12,28,15,23$ for the four sets in order ($\rho_{\min}^2=26,26,9410,
1018082$).  A block of degree at least $d_0$ has
$\Lambda\ge d_0\log(\rho_{\min}/2)$, which exceeds $11.23$, $26.20$,
$58.22$ and $143.1$ respectively (Theorem~\ref{thm:gausscharge}).  A block $B$ of degree at most $d_0-1$ has,
by Lemma~\ref{lem:rowmono},
$\Lambda(B)\ge\log|\operatorname{lead}B|+\sum_{k\le K}\log^+b_k(B)
\ge\sum_{k\le K}\log^+\beta_k(d_0-1)$, the $K$ largest nonleading magnitudes
sitting at distinct positions.  For each $k$ and each set $U$ of $k-1$
excluded positions, $\min\max_{j\notin U}|f_j|$ over monic multiples of degree
$d_0-1$ is a linear program with rational data, which we solved in exact
rational arithmetic, each optimum re-certified by a weak-duality
certificate.  The minima over $U$ are
\[
  \beta_1(11)\ge1304.02,\quad\beta_2(11)\ge50.73
\]
for the first set, so $\Lambda(B)\ge\log(1304.02\cdot50.73)>11.09$, and
\[
  \beta_1(14)\ge1.0189\cdot10^{12},\quad\beta_2(14)\ge3.931\cdot10^8,\quad
  \beta_3(14)\ge9.878\cdot10^7
\]
for the third, so $\Lambda(B)>65.8$.  For the second set,
$\beta_1(27)\ge161134$, $\beta_2(27)\ge6652.3$, $\beta_3(27)\ge504.6$, so
$\Lambda(B)>27.01$; for the fourth,
$\beta_1(22)\ge1.1308\cdot10^{24}$, $\beta_2(22)\ge4.386\cdot10^{18}$,
$\beta_3(22)\ge1.082\cdot10^{18}$, so $\Lambda(B)>139.8$.  Lemma~\ref{lem:gaussblocks} gives
$\Lambda(F)\ge\Lambda(B)$ for any block $B$ of $F$.
\end{proof}

At these roots the rows exceed the imaginary rows of
Corollary~\ref{cor:imag}, some by far: $\beta_3(14)\approx9.9\cdot10^7$
against $c_{(3)}^2-1=10608$ for the second set, and for $1\pm5i$, $-2\pm7i$,
$3\pm11i$ the exact value $\beta_3(27)\ge504.6$ exceeds $c_{(3)}^2-1=120$,
and floating-point linear programs up to degree $12$ give a fourth
coefficient near $124$, which three purely imaginary pairs never force.  The bottleneck in the certificates is
the degree-charging side, not the rows.

\begin{proposition}[No exact transfer at register-type pairs]
\label{prop:gaussnotransfer}
Let $\alpha=a+ic\in\mathbb Z[i]$ with $ac\ne0$ and $|a|\ne|c|$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item No power $\alpha^m$, $m\ge1$, is real.  Hence no real-rooted
$R\in\R[y]$ and $m\ge1$ have $(x-\alpha)\mid R(x^m)$:
Theorem~\ref{thm:transfer} cannot turn such a pair into real roots.
\item If $\alpha'=a'+ic'$ has $0<|a|<c$, $0<|a'|<c'$, and $c,c'$ are powers of
distinct primes, then $\alpha,\alpha'$ do not lie on a common ray, so
Corollary~\ref{cor:ray} does not apply to the two pairs together.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
1.  If $\alpha^m$ were real, $(\alpha/\bar\alpha)^m=1$, so $\alpha/\bar\alpha$
would be a root of unity in $\mathbb Q(i)$, hence one of $\pm1,\pm i$.  The
values $\pm1$ make $\alpha$ real or purely imaginary; $\pm i$ make
$\alpha^2=\pm i|\alpha|^2$, so $a^2=c^2$.  All are excluded.  A root $\alpha$
of $R(x^m)$ has $\alpha^m$ a root of $R$.

2.  A common ray means $a/c=a'/c'$, so $ac'=a'c$; as $\gcd(c,c')=1$, $c\mid a$,
contradicting $0<|a|<c$.
\end{proof}

In Polynomial's construction the register operands satisfy $|a|\le A$, and
all but $O(\pi(A))$ register roots $a\pm p^bi$ have $p>A$.  Among those,
two roots with distinct primes are covered by item~2, and two with one prime
and exponents $b<b'$ would need $a'=ap^{b'-b}$, impossible for
$0<|a|,|a'|\le A<p$.  So neither exact reduction of this section reaches
them.

The remaining results of this subsection record what joint integrality at
several roots does and does not give.  Lemma~\ref{lem:gausswindow} rewrites
cleanliness in terms of the cofactor $F/P$, Lemma~\ref{lem:gausspartial} is
the several-root form of Lemma~\ref{lem:gausscarry}, and
Proposition~\ref{prop:carrylimit} shows that carry sizes alone cannot give more
than linear mass.  Lemma~\ref{lem:gaussround} identifies the rows with a
problem about a rounding recurrence.  Theorem~\ref{thm:padicmass} is an
unconditional mass bound from $q$-adic Newton polygons; it pays the charge
$\sum_jj\log\rho_j$, up to a factor at most $4$, for Gaussian pairs of one
common prime-power norm
(Corollary~\ref{cor:padiccircle}), the configuration that the angle mechanism
of Proposition~\ref{prop:noangle} would need, but only a linear charge for
pairwise coprime norms.  Proposition~\ref{prop:gaussrowsfail} shows that the
rows need the register hypothesis.

\begin{lemma}[Cofactor windows]\label{lem:gausswindow}
Let $F=PQ$ with $Q=\sum_tq_tx^t\in\mathbb Z[x]$ and $Q(0)\ne0$; put $q_t=0$
for $t<0$ and for $t>\deg Q$, and let $D=\deg F$ and $1\le m\le D$.  The
polynomial
\[
  E_m=\sum_{t=m-2K}^{m-1}q_t\sum_{s=m-t}^{2K}p_s\,x^{s+t-m}\ \in\mathbb Z[x],
  \qquad P=\sum_{s=0}^{2K}p_sx^s,
\]
has degree less than $2K$ and depends only on $q_{m-2K},\ldots,q_{m-1}$, and
\[
  G_m=x^{-m}P\sum_{t\ge m}q_tx^t+E_m,\qquad G_m(\alpha_j)=E_m(\alpha_j)\quad(1\le j\le K).
\]
The split $m$ is $P$-clean if and only if $q_{m-2K}=\cdots=q_{m-1}=0$.  Hence
$F$ is $P$-irreducible if and only if $Q$ has no $2K$ consecutive zero
coefficients $q_t,\ldots,q_{t+2K-1}$ with $0<t$ and $t+2K-1<\deg Q$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write $Q_{<m}=\sum_{t<m}q_tx^t$.  Since $P\,(Q-Q_{<m})$ has no terms below
$x^m$, the terms of $F$ from $x^m$ on are those of $P(Q-Q_{<m})$ plus those
of $PQ_{<m}$, and the latter are
$\sum_{t<m}q_t\sum_{s\ge m-t}p_sx^{s+t}=x^mE_m$; the constraint $s\le2K$ forces
$t\ge m-2K$, and $s+t-m\le2K-1$.  This is the displayed formula, and
$P(\alpha_j)=0$ gives $G_m(\alpha_j)=E_m(\alpha_j)$.  The split is $P$-clean
if and only if $E_m$ vanishes at the $2K$ distinct roots of $P$, that is, if and
only if $E_m=0$, as $\deg E_m<2K$.  The coefficient of $x^{2K-1-r}$ in $E_m$
($0\le r<2K$) is $q_{m-1-r}p_{2K}$ plus a combination of
$q_{m-r},\ldots,q_{m-1}$, and $p_{2K}=1$; so $E_m=0$ if and only if
$q_{m-1}=\cdots=q_{m-2K}=0$.  For $1\le m\le D$ the window
$\{m-2K,\ldots,m-1\}$ meets $\{0,\ldots,\deg Q\}$, and it contains no
nonzero coefficient exactly when it avoids $0$ and $\deg Q$ (where
$q_0,q_{\deg Q}\ne0$) and $Q$ vanishes on it.  Finally $F(0)=P(0)Q(0)\ne0$.
\end{proof}

So the blocks of Lemma~\ref{lem:gaussblocks} are the pieces of $Q$ between
runs of at least $2K$ zero coefficients, and the carry vector
$(G_m(\alpha_j))_j$ at a split is the image of a window of $2K$ consecutive
coefficients of $Q$ under a fixed injective linear map.

\begin{lemma}[Partially clean splits]\label{lem:gausspartial}
Let $F\in\mathbb Z[x]$ be divisible by $P$, let $1\le m\le\deg F$, let
$J=\{j:G_m(\alpha_j)\ne0\}$, and put
$P_{J^c}=\prod_{l\notin J}(x-\alpha_l)(x-\bar\alpha_l)$.  Then $P_{J^c}$
divides $R_m$ and $G_m$ in $\mathbb Z[x]$, and for every $j\in J$
\[
  |G_m(\alpha_j)|\ \ge\ |P_{J^c}(\alpha_j)|
  =\prod_{l\notin J}|\alpha_j-\alpha_l|\,|\alpha_j-\bar\alpha_l|
  \ \ge\ \prod_{l\notin J}(c_j+c_l),
  \qquad |R_m(\alpha_j)|\ \ge\ \rho_j^m\,|P_{J^c}(\alpha_j)|.
\]
If moreover $\rho_j>2$, some $i<m$ has
$|f_i|\ge(\rho_j/2)^{m-i}\,|P_{J^c}(\alpha_j)|$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For $l\notin J$, $G_m(\alpha_l)=0$ and, $G_m$ being real,
$G_m(\bar\alpha_l)=0$; these $2|J^c|$ roots are distinct, and $P_{J^c}$ is
monic in $\mathbb Z[x]$, so division with remainder gives $G_m=P_{J^c}H$ with
$H\in\mathbb Z[x]$.  Also $R_m=F-x^mG_m$ vanishes at the same points, so
$P_{J^c}\mid R_m$.  For $j\in J$, $H(\alpha_j)\in\mathbb Z[i]$ and
$H(\alpha_j)\ne0$ because $G_m(\alpha_j)\ne0$; so $|H(\alpha_j)|\ge1$ and
$|G_m(\alpha_j)|\ge|P_{J^c}(\alpha_j)|$.  Distinct Gaussian integers have
$|\alpha_j-\alpha_l|\ge1$, and
$|\alpha_j-\bar\alpha_l|=|(a_j-a_l)+i(c_j+c_l)|\ge c_j+c_l$.  The bound on
$R_m$ is $R_m(\alpha_j)=-\alpha_j^mG_m(\alpha_j)$, and the last claim follows
from it exactly as in Lemma~\ref{lem:gausscarry}.
\end{proof}

This is the form in which integrality at several roots enters a single split.
The product $\prod_{j\in J}|G_m(\alpha_j)|^2=\prod_{j\in J}N(G_m(\alpha_j))$ is
a nonzero integer, but its lower bound $1$ already follows from the factors
one at a time.  The gain comes only from the roots at which the split
\emph{is} clean, and it is absent at splits that are clean at no root, which
is the case of $P$ itself and of every multiple built from it without gaps.

\begin{proposition}[What carry sizes cannot see]\label{prop:carrylimit}
Let $d\ge1$, and let numbers $1\le W_j(m)\le(2\rho_{\max})^{2K}$ be given for
$1\le m\le d$ and $1\le j\le K$.  Put $W=\lceil\max_{j,m}W_j(m)\rceil$ and
$Z=\lceil W\rho_{\max}^d\rceil$.  The polynomial $\tilde F=Z+Wx^d$, whose parts
at the split $m$ are $\tilde R_m=Z$ and $\tilde G_m=Wx^{d-m}$, satisfies
\[
  |\tilde R_m(\alpha_j)|\ge\rho_j^m\,W_j(m),\qquad
  |\tilde G_m(\alpha_j)|\ge W_j(m)\qquad(1\le m\le d,\ 1\le j\le K),
\]
and $\Lambda(\tilde F)\le(d+4K+3)\log(2\rho_{\max})$.  The factors
$|P_{J^c}(\alpha_j)|$ of Lemma~\ref{lem:gausspartial} are admissible $W_j(m)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
$|\tilde R_m(\alpha_j)|=Z\ge W\rho_{\max}^d\ge\rho_j^mW_j(m)$ and
$|\tilde G_m(\alpha_j)|=W\rho_j^{d-m}\ge W_j(m)$.  Next $Z\le2W\rho_{\max}^d$
and $W\le2(2\rho_{\max})^{2K}$, so
$\Lambda(\tilde F)=\log Z+\log W\le d\log\rho_{\max}+4K\log(2\rho_{\max})+3\log2$.
Finally
$|P_{J^c}(\alpha_j)|\le\prod_l(\rho_j+\rho_l)^2\le(2\rho_{\max})^{2K}$.
\end{proof}

$\tilde F$ is not a multiple of $P$.  The point is that every consequence of
Lemmas~\ref{lem:gausscarry} and~\ref{lem:gausspartial} that uses only these
inequalities, such as the charging in Theorem~\ref{thm:gausscharge} or a
lower bound for $\max_{i<m}|f_i|$ at each split, holds for $\tilde F$ too.  So
it gives at most $O((d+K)\log\rho_{\max})$, which is linear in $K$ for
blocks of degree $O(K)$, while Corollary~\ref{cor:gaussfinite} shows that
blocks of degree $O(K^2)$ are the only ones that matter.  Such per-split bounds
up to $P(0)$ are moreover witnessed by a single coefficient, since
$|f_0|\ge P(0)=\prod_j\rho_j^2$ whenever $F(0)\ne0$.  A mass bound of order
$\sum_jj\log\rho_j$ has to use the exact arithmetic of $F$, not only the
sizes of its carries.

The exact arithmetic is a rounding recurrence.  Write $F=PQ$ with
$Q(0)\ne0$.  Then $f_m=P(0)q_m+\sum_{s=1}^{2K}p_sq_{m-s}$ for every $m$, so a
small coefficient $f_m$ determines $q_m$ from the $2K$ coefficients below it.

\begin{lemma}[Rounding rigidity]\label{lem:gaussround}
Let $F=PQ$ with $Q\in\mathbb Z[x]$, $Q(0)\ne0$, $D=\deg F$, and put $q_t=0$ for
$t<0$ and $t>\deg Q$.  If $0<m\le D$ and $|f_m|<P(0)/2$, then $q_m$ is the
integer nearest to $-P(0)^{-1}\sum_{s=1}^{2K}p_sq_{m-s}$.  In particular, if
$b_2(F)<X\le P(0)/2$, then $F=P\,Q_t$, where $t=q_0$ and $Q_t$ is the
orbit of the recurrence
\[
  q_m=\Bigl\lfloor-\frac1{P(0)}\sum_{s=1}^{2K}p_sq_{m-s}\Bigr\rceil
  \qquad(m\ge1),\qquad q_{-1}=\cdots=q_{-2K}=0,\ q_0=t,
\]
stopped at its first run of $2K$ zeros; here $\lfloor y\rceil$ is an integer
nearest to $y$ (a tie gives a residual $P(0)/2\ge X$, so it does not matter
how ties are broken).  Conversely, every $t\ne0$ whose orbit
reaches $2K$ consecutive zeros and all of whose residuals
$r_m=\sum_{s=0}^{2K}p_sq_{m-s}$, $0<m<\deg(PQ_t)$, satisfy $|r_m|<X$ gives a
multiple $PQ_t$ with $b_2<X$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The identity for $f_m$ is the convolution $F=PQ$ with $p_0=P(0)$.  If
$|f_m|<P(0)/2$, then $q_m$ lies within less than $\frac12$ of
$-P(0)^{-1}\sum_{s\ge1}p_sq_{m-s}$, which determines it.  If
$b_2(F)<X\le P(0)/2$, at most one nonleading coefficient has modulus at
least $X$, and $|f_0|=P(0)|q_0|\ge P(0)$ is one; so every $f_m$ with
$0<m<D$ is below $P(0)/2$, and $q_1,q_2,\ldots$ are the orbit.  A window of
$2K$ zeros makes every later term zero, and $q_{\deg Q}\ne0$, so the first
such run starts at $\deg Q+1$.  The coefficients of $PQ_t$ at $0<m<\deg(PQ_t)$ are the
residuals, which gives the converse.
\end{proof}

So the second row at Gaussian pairs is a Diophantine question about one
contracting integer recurrence (a symmetric shift radix system with
parameter $(p_{2K},\ldots,p_1)/P(0)$): how small can all its residuals be
along an orbit that dies?  Enumerating $1\le t\le2\cdot10^5$ (exact
arithmetic; $-t$ gives $-F$) gives the least value $b_2=51$ at
$1\pm5i,\ -2\pm7i$, attained at $t=20$ in degree $6$ (the exact rational LP
of Proposition~\ref{prop:gaussinst} gives $\beta_2(11)\ge50.73$), and for
$1\pm97i,\ -2\pm101i$ the minimum over this range is $b_2(P)=19607$, against
$14715$ for $\pm97i,\ \pm101i$ and the imaginary row $101^2-1=10200$.  For
$1\pm97i,\ -2\pm101i,\ 3\pm103i$ and $t\le5\cdot10^4$ it is
$b_2(P)=393226160$, which matches $\beta_2(14)\ge3.931\cdot10^8$ of
Proposition~\ref{prop:gaussinst}.  Such enumerations do not certify a
bound, since $t$ is unbounded.

Newton polygons give an unconditional bound that uses divisibility instead
of carries.  For a prime $q$ fix an extension $w$ of the $q$-adic valuation to
$\overline{\mathbb Q}$, normalized by $w(q)=1$.

\begin{theorem}[Mass from $q$-adic Newton polygons]\label{thm:padicmass}
Let $F\in\mathbb Z[x]$ be nonzero and let $\beta_1,\ldots,\beta_n$ be nonzero
roots of $F$, each listed at most as often as its multiplicity.  Let
$u_1<\cdots<u_r$ be the distinct positive values among the $w(\beta_i)$, and
$M_s=\sum_{i:\,w(\beta_i)=u_s}w(\beta_i)$.  Then $F$ has nonzero coefficients
at $r+1$ distinct positions $y_1>\cdots>y_{r+1}$ with
$v_q(f_{y_t})\ge\sum_{s<t}M_s$, so
\[
  \sum_{i:\,f_i\ne0}v_q(f_i)\ \ge\ \sum_{s=1}^r(r+1-s)\,M_s .
\]
Summing over the primes,
\[
  \Lambda(F)\ \ge\ \sum_q\log q\sum_{s=1}^{r_q}(r_q+1-s)\,M_{q,s}.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $N$ be the lower convex hull of the points $(i,v_q(f_i))$, $f_i\ne0$;
its vertices are such points.  Removing a power of $x$ only translates $N$,
so we may read the theorem below for $F/x^e$, $e$ the order of $F$ at $0$.  By the Newton polygon theorem over the
completion of $\overline{\mathbb Q}$ at $w$, a segment of $N$ of slope $-u$ and
horizontal length $\ell$ corresponds to exactly $\ell$ roots of $F$ (with
multiplicity) of valuation $u$, and slopes increase from left to right.  For
$1\le t\le r$ let $y_t$ be the vertex at which the valuations of the
segments change from at least $u_t$ (on the left) to less than $u_t$ (on the
right), with $y_t=D$ if there is no segment on the right, and let $y_{r+1}$ be
the leftmost vertex.  A segment of valuation exactly $u_t$ exists, and it lies
to the right of $y_{t+1}$ and to the left of $y_t$; hence
$y_1>\cdots>y_{r+1}$.  Reading $N$ from the right end,
\[
  v_q(f_{y_t})=N(y_t)=v_q(f_D)+\sum_{\text{roots }\gamma,\ w(\gamma)<u_t}w(\gamma)
  =N(y_0)+\sum_{\text{roots }\gamma,\ 0\le w(\gamma)<u_t}w(\gamma),
\]
where $y_0$ is the vertex separating negative from nonnegative valuations
($y_0=D$ if there is none), so that $N(y_0)=v_q(f_{y_0})\ge0$.  The roots of
$F$ include the $\beta_i$, so the last sum is at least $\sum_{s<t}M_s$ (for
$t=r+1$ it is at least $\sum_{s\le r}M_s$).  Summing over $t$ gives the
displayed inequality.  For the mass, $\log|f_i|\ge\sum_qv_q(f_i)\log q$ for
every nonzero $f_i\in\mathbb Z$, so
$\Lambda(F)\ge\sum_i\sum_qv_q(f_i)\log q$, and the inner sums over each $q$
are bounded as above.
\end{proof}

For a multiple $F$ of $P$ take the $\beta_i$ to be the $2K$ roots
$\alpha_j,\bar\alpha_j$.  For $q\equiv1\pmod4$, $q=\mathfrak q\bar{\mathfrak q}$,
choose $w$ through $\mathfrak q$, so $w(\alpha)=v_{\mathfrak q}(\alpha)$ and
$w(\bar\alpha)=v_{\bar{\mathfrak q}}(\alpha)$; for $q\equiv3\pmod4$,
$w(\alpha_j)=w(\bar\alpha_j)=v_q(\gcd(a_j,c_j))$; for $q=2$,
$w(\alpha)=\frac12v_{1+i}(\alpha)$.  When the norms
$N_j=a_j^2+c_j^2$ are pairwise coprime and the $\alpha_j$ primitive, every
$r_q\le1$ and the theorem gives $\Lambda(F)\ge\sum_j\log N_j$, the first
row.  It is strong when many roots share a prime with different valuations.
